The ℓ p \ell^{p} -boundedness of wave operators for the fourth order Schrödinger operators on the lattice ℤ \mathbb{Z} Thanks: The work is partially supported by NSFC No.12171182, 12531005 and and the Fundamental Research Funds for the Central Universities
Sisi Huang and Xiaohua Yao

Abstract
. This paper investigates the ℓ p \ell^{p} boundedness of wave operators W ± ​ ( H , Δ 2 ) W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2}) associated with discrete fourth-order Schrödinger operators H = Δ 2 + V H=\Delta^{2}+V on the lattice ℤ \mathbb{Z} , where ( Δ ​ ϕ ) ​ ( n ) = ϕ ⁡ ( n + 1 ) + ϕ ⁡ ( n − 1 ) − 2 ​ ϕ ​ ( n ) , n ∈ ℤ , (\Delta\phi)(n)=\phi(n+1)+\phi(n-1)-2\phi(n),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}, and V ⁡ ( n ) V(n) is a real-valued potential on ℤ \mathbb{Z} . Under suitable decay assumptions on V V (depending on the types of zero resonance of H H ), we show that the wave operators W ± ​ ( H , Δ 2 ) W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2}) are bounded on ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) \ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}) for all 1 < p < ∞ 1<p<\infty : ‖ W ± ​ ( H , Δ 2 ) ​ f ‖ ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) ≲ ‖ f ‖ ℓ p ​ ( ℤ ) . \|W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})f\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})}. In particular, if both thresholds 0 0 and 16 16 are regular points of H H , we prove that W ± ​ ( H , Δ 2 ) W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2}) are neither bounded on the endpoint space ℓ 1 ​ ( ℤ ) \ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}) nor on ℓ ∞ ​ ( ℤ ) \ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}) . We remark that the proof of these bounds relies fundamentally on the asymptotic expansions of the resolvent of H H near the thresholds 0 0 and 16 16 , and on the theory of discrete singular integrals on the lattice. As applications, we derive the following sharp ℓ p − ℓ p ′ \ell^{p}-\ell^{p^{\prime}} decay estimates for solutions to the discrete beam equation with a parameter a ∈ ℝ a\in\mathbb{R} on the lattice ℤ \mathbb{Z} : ‖ cos ⁡ ( t ​ H + a 2 ) ​ P a ​ c ​ ( H ) ‖ ℓ p → ℓ p ′ + ‖ sin ⁡ ( t ​ H + a 2 ) t ​ H + a 2 ​ P a ​ c ​ ( H ) ‖ ℓ p → ℓ p ′ ≲ | t | − 1 3 ​ ( 1 p − 1 p ′ ) , t ≠ 0 , \|{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}+\left\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{H+a^{2}}}P_{ac}(H)\right\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}})},\quad t\neq 0, where 1 < p ≤ 2 1<p\leq 2 , p ′ {p^{\prime}} is the conjugated index of p p and P a ​ c ​ ( H ) P_{ac}(H) denotes the spectral projection onto the absolutely continuous spectrum space of H H .



1.
Introduction and main results

1.1.
Introduction

Let
\Delta
denote the discrete Laplacian on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
, defined by

(\Delta\phi)(n)=\phi(n+1)+\phi(n-1)-2\phi(n),\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(1.1)

We consider the following fourth-order Schrödinger operators
H
acting on the space
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
:

H=\Delta^{2}+V,

(1.2)

where
V(n)
is a real-valued potential on
\mathbb{Z}
and satisfies
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
with
\left<n\right>=(1+|n|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Both
\Delta^{2}
and
H
are bounded self-adjoint operators on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, generating the associated unitary groups
e^{it\Delta^{2}}
and
e^{itH}
, respectively.

The
wave operators
associated with
H
are defined as the strong limits on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
:

W_{\pm}:=W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})=s-\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\mp\infty}e^{itH}e^{-it\Delta^{2}}.

(1.3)

When
\beta>1
, it is known (cf.
[
50
, Section XI.3]
) that (
1.3
) exist as partial isometries from
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
to
\mathcal{H}_{ac}(H)
(the absolutely continuous spectral subspace of
H
) and are asymptotically complete (i.e., Ran
W_{+}=
Ran
W_{-}=\mathcal{H}_{ac}(H)
). Moreover, the
inverse
(
dual
)
wave operators

W_{\pm}(\Delta^{2},H)=s-\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\mp\infty}e^{it\Delta^{2}}e^{-itH}P_{ac}(H)

also exist and satisfy
W_{\pm}(\Delta^{2},H)=W^{*}_{\pm}
, where
P_{ac}(H)
denotes the spectral projection onto
\mathcal{H}_{ac}(H)
.

Such wave operators, initially introduced in quantum scattering theory by Moller and Friedrichs and later developed by Jauch, Cook and Kato etc.,
serve as indispensable tools for understanding the long-time behavior of evolution equations, cf.
[
35
,
50
,
5
]
. Owing to their fundamental role in scattering theory, non-linear partial differential equations, and spectral theory, the study of wave operators occupies an important position in modern mathematical physics. In particular, the analysis of their
L^{p}
-boundedness
has drawn growing interest and achieved substantial progress. This paper aims to investigate the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of
W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})
and
W^{*}_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})
associated with (
1.2
).

In Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, it is known that the study of
L^{p}
boundedness of wave operators was initiated by K. Yajima in his seminal works
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
]

for second-order Schrödinger operators
-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+V
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 3
, where he proved that wave operators are
L^{p}
bounded for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
if zero energy is regular. Subsequently, this topic has been developed for lower dimensions, for instance, Jensen-Yajima
[
61
,
34
]
, Erdoğan-Goldberg-Green
[
21
]
for
d=2
and Weder
[
56
]
, Galtbayar-Yajima
[
26
]
, D’Ancona-Fanelli
[
16
]
for
d=1
and references therein.

Moreover, significant advances of this issue have been made for the higher-order Schrödinger operators
(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{d}})^{m}+V
with
m\geq 2
, although which has only begun in very recent several years, compared to the second-order case studied since the 1990s. The first work
[
25
]
by Goldberg and Green established
L^{p}
boundedness for
1<p<\infty
in the regular case for
(m,d)=(2,3)
, which was subsequently extended to general case
d>2m
by Erdoğan and Green in
[
19
,
20
]
. Further developments include Mizutani, Wan and Yao’s investigation
[
45
,
46
]
of endpoint behavior and zero energy resonances for
(m,d)=(2,3)
and their complete analysis
[
44
]
of all zero resonance types in
(m,d)=(2,1)
, along with Galtbayar-Yajima’s study
[
27
]
of the
(m,d)=(2,4)
case. More recently, Erdoğan-Green-LaMaster
[
23
]
considered the case
d>4m
while Cheng, Soffer, Wu and Yao
[
11
,
12
]
covered the remaining cases
1\leq d\leq 4m
.

As a natural extension, the scattering theory on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has attracted much attention in recent two decades and undergone significant developments (cf.
[
6
,
3
,
2
,
30
,
29
,
10
]
). In contrast, the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of wave operators on lattices seems largely unexplored. To the best of our knowledge, the only known result
is due to Cuccagna
[
13
]
, who established the
\ell^{p}
bounds of wave operators for
-\Delta+V
on
\mathbb{Z}
. The corresponding problem for higher-order wave operators, even for
H=\Delta^{2}+V
on
\mathbb{Z}
, remains open, which consists of
the main goal of this paper.

Furthermore, interestingly, once we have established the
\ell^{p}
-boundedness of
W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})
and
W^{*}_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})
, the following
intertwining property

f(H)P_{ac}(H)=W_{\pm}f(\Delta^{2})W^{*}_{\pm}

(1.4)

enables us to reduce
\ell^{p}-\ell^{q}
estimates for the perturbed operator
f(H)
to the corresponding estimates for the free operator
f(\Delta^{2})
:

\|f(H)P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{q}}\leq\|W_{\pm}\|_{\ell^{q}\rightarrow\ell^{q}}\|f(\Delta^{2})\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{q}}\|W^{*}_{\pm}\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p}},

where
f
is any Borel function on
\mathbb{R}
.

As applications, we will establish the time decay estimates for the solution to the discrete beam equation with parameter
a\in\mathbb{R}
on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\partial_{tt}u)(t,n)+\big((H+a^{2})u\big)(t,n)=0,\ \ (t,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z},\\
&u(0,n)=\varphi_{1}(n),\ \left(\partial_{t}u\right)(0,n)=\varphi_{2}(n),\end{aligned}\right.

(1.5)

whose solution is given by

u_{a}(t,n)={\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})\varphi_{1}(n)+\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})}{\sqrt{H+a^{2}}}\varphi_{2}(n).

Note that in the free case (i.e.,
V\equiv 0
), by means of Fourier method, the following sharp
\ell^{p}-\ell^{p^{\prime}}
decay estimates hold for all
a\in\mathbb{R}
(see Theorem
7.1
below):

\|{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}})\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}+\left\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}\right\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}})},\quad t\neq 0,

where
1\leq p\leq 2
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
.
When
V\not\equiv 0
, the decay estimates for the solution operators of equation (
1.5
) are affected by the spectrum of
H
, which in turn depends on the conditions of potential
V
. In this paper, assuming that the potential
V
has fast decaying and
H
has no embedded positive eigenvalues in the continuous spectrum interval
(0,16)
, we prove that the wave operators
W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})
are bounded on
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})
for all
1<p<\infty
(see Theorem
1.3
below). As a consequence of this boundedness and the intertwining property (
1.4
), we obtain the following
\ell^{p}-\ell^{p^{\prime}}
decay estimates:

\|{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}+\left\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{H+a^{2}}}P_{ac}(H)\right\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}})},\quad t\neq 0,

for all
1<p\leq 2
and
a\in\mathbb{R}
. For more details, we refer to Section
7
.

To obtain the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of
W_{\pm}(H,\Delta^{2})
, our starting point is the stationary representation of
W_{\pm}
. We first establish the limiting absorption principle for the operator
H
, and then study the asymptotic expansions of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda)
near thresholds
0
and
16
for all resonance types (see Definition
1.1
below). Finally, we employ the Schur test lemma and the theory of discrete singular integral on the lattice to derive the desired boundedness.

1.2.
Main results

In this subsection, we present our main results. First, we give some definitions. Unlike the continuous case where zero is the only critical value, our discrete setting will involve two critical values:
0
(degenerate) and
16
(non-degenerate) (see Subsection
2.1
). This gives rise to more diverse resonance types, which can be characterized respectively by the solutions to difference equations
H\phi=0
and
H\phi=16\phi
in some intersection spaces

W_{\sigma}(\mathbb{Z})
:=
\underset{s>\sigma}{\bigcap}\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z})
with
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
and

\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z})=\Big\{\phi=\left\{\phi(n)\right\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}:\|\phi\|^{2}_{\ell^{2,s}}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\left<n\right>^{2s}|\phi(n)|^{2}<\infty\Big\}.

More precisely, let
a\lesssim b
denote
a\leq cb
with some constant
c>0
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
, we have

Definition 1.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
be defined on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
and
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
.

(I) Classification of resonances at threshold 0

(i)

0
is a regular point of
H
if
H\phi=0
has only zero solution in
W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii)

0
is a first kind resonance of
H
if
H\phi=0
has nonzero solution in
W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
but only zero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iii)

0
is a second kind resonance of
H
if
H\phi=0
has nonzero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
but only zero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iv)

0
is an eigenvalue of
H
if
H\phi=0
has nonzero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(II) Classification of resonances at threshold 16

(i)

16
is a regular point of
H
if
H\phi=16\phi
has only zero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii)

16
is a resonance of
H
if
H\phi=16\phi
has nonzero solution in
W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
but only zero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iii)

16
is an eigenvalue of
H
if
H\phi=16\phi
has nonzero solution in
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Obviously, when
V\equiv 0
, both 0 and 16 are the resonances of
H
. This can be verified by taking
\phi_{1}(n)=cn+d
and
\phi_{2}(n)=(-1)^{n}c
with
c\neq 0
, which satisfy
\Delta^{2}\phi_{1}=0
and
\Delta^{2}\phi_{2}=16\phi_{2}
. Beyond this special case, there are some other non-trivial zero/sixteen resonance examples.

1. Example of resonance.
Consider the function
\phi(n)=2
for
n=0
and
\phi(n)=1
for
n\neq 0
and define the potentials
V_{1}(n)=-\frac{(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)}{\phi(n)}
and
V_{2}(n)=16+V_{1}(n)
. Then

(\Delta^{2}+V_{1})\phi=0,\quad(\Delta^{2}+V_{2})\phi=16\phi.

In this case, we have

V_{1}(n)=\left\{\begin{aligned} -3,&\quad n=0,\\
4,&\quad n=\pm 1,\\
-1,&\quad n=\pm 2,\\
0,&\quad{\rm else},\end{aligned}\right.,\quad V_{2}(n)=\left\{\begin{aligned} 13,&\quad n=0,\\
20,&\quad n=\pm 1,\\
15,&\quad n=\pm 2,\\
16,&\quad{\rm else}.\end{aligned}\right.

It indicates that
0
persists a resonance even for such compactly supported potential, and by shifting the potential by
16
one can turns the resonance at
0
into a resonance at
16
.

2. Example of eigenvalue.
Take
\phi(n)=(1+n^{2})^{-s}
with
s>\frac{1}{4}
,
V_{1}(n)=-\frac{(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)}{\phi(n)}
and
V_{2}(n)=16+V_{1}(n)
. At this time,

V_{1}(n)=O(\left<n\right>^{-4}),\quad|n|\rightarrow\infty.

This implies that
0
becomes an eigenvalue of
H
under such slowly decaying potential. However, for potentials exhibiting faster decay with
\beta>9
, we can preclude the zero eigenvalue case. A detailed explanation of this can be found in
[
28
, Lemma 5.2]
.

Remark 1.2
.

We remark that zero (resp. sixteen) resonance are closely related to the asymptotic expansions of the resolvent
R_{V}(z)
of the operator
H
near zero (resp. sixteen) energy, and are further connected to the asymptotic expansions of
M^{-1}(\mu)
near
\mu=0
(resp.
\mu=2
) through the formula (
2.6
).

For instance, the concepts of these zero resonances originate from the invertibility of specific operators
T_{j}

restricted to the ranges of orthogonal projection operators

S_{j}
on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
for
j=0,1,2
during the computation of
M^{-1}(\mu)
near
\mu=0
. This invertibility is equivalent to whether the corresponding kernel subspace

{\rm Ker}T_{j}\big|_{S_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
is non-trivial, which further manifests as whether the associated projection space
S_{j+1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is non-trivial.
The non-triviality of these projection spaces
S_{j+1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
in turn is equivalent to the existence of non-zero solutions to difference equation
H\phi=0
in a suitable weighted space
W_{\sigma}(\mathbb{Z})

(see Subsection
2.2
below for more details).

We now illustrate the main result. Denote by
\mathbb{B}(X,Y)
the space of all bounded linear operators from
X
to
Y
and abbreviate
\mathbb{B}(X,X)
as
\mathbb{B}(X)
when
X=Y
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
. If

\displaystyle\beta>\left\{\begin{aligned} &17,\ 0\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&19,\ 0\ is\ a\ first\ kind\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&27,\ 0\ is\ a\ second\ kind\ resonance\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(1.6)

then the wave operators
W_{\pm}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1<p<\infty
.

Furthermore, we establish the following unboundedness results at the endpoints.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
, and suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
.

(i)
If both
0
and
16
are regular points of
H
and
\beta>15
, then
W_{\pm}\notin\mathbb{B}(\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}))\cup\mathbb{B}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}))
.

(ii)
Let
V
be compactly supported. Then

•

If
0
is a regular point and
16
is a
(
an
)
resonance or eigenvalue of
H
, then
W_{\pm}\not\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}))
. Moreover,
W_{\pm}\notin\mathbb{B}(\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}))
given that in addition

\displaystyle 16(1\mp 3\sqrt{2})\neq\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{C}_{1},\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&\mathcal{C}_{2},\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are constants given in (
6.7
) and (
6.8
), respectively.

•

If
0
is a first kind resonance of
H
and
\mathcal{C}_{3}\neq 0
, then
W_{\pm}\not\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})).
Moreover,
W_{\pm}\not\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}))
given additionally that

\displaystyle 192|D|\neq\left\{\begin{aligned} &16,\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{1}\big|,\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{2}\big|,\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{C}_{3}
and
D
are constants defined in (
6.11
) and (
6.12
), respectively.

•

If
0
is a second kind resonance of
H
and
\mathcal{C}_{4}\neq 0
, then
W_{\pm}\not\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}))
. Moreover,
W_{\pm}\not\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}))
given additionally that

\displaystyle 192|E|\neq\left\{\begin{aligned} &16,\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{1}\big|,\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{2}\big|,\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mathcal{C}_{4}
and
E
are constants defined in (
6.16
) and (
6.17
), respectively.

Remark 1.5
.

Further remarks on Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.4
are given as follows.

(1)

Although two resonance types of
H
may coexist, we emphasize that the decay rate of the potential
V
in (
1.6
) above is fundamentally determined by the types of zero energy. The required rate
\beta
of
V
in Theorem
1.3
, derived from the asymptotical expansion of
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
(see Lemma
2.8
below), might not be optimal.

(2)

We point out that the coefficients
\mathcal{C}_{j}
,
D
and
E
above are closely related to the operators in our expansions of
M^{-1}(\mu)
.

(3)

Our approach of proofs of Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
is motivated by the recent work of Mizutani, Wan and Yao
[
44
]
on the continuous counterpart of this problem. However, some distinctive challenges arise in our discrete setting. First, the diversity of resonance types significantly complicates our boundedness analysis, particularly concerning endpoint unboundedness. Second, establishing
\ell^{p}
-boundedness in our framework involves
the discrete singular integral theory on lattices
.

Remark 1.6
.

As an application of Theorem
1.3
and the intertwining property (
1.4
), it is known that the following
\ell^{p}-\ell^{p^{\prime}}
decay estimates for solutions to the discrete beam equation (
1.5
) with a parameter
a\in\mathbb{R}
hold:

\|{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}+\left\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{H+a^{2}}}P_{ac}(H)\right\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}})},

(1.7)

where
t\neq 0
,
1<p\leq 2
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
.
We remark that when
a=0
, this estimate remains valid even for the endpoint case
p=1
, as established in our previous work
[
28
]
through a direct approach. While the unboundedness of the wave operators on
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
here is not valid to yield this decay estimate for the endpoint case
p=1
, it nevertheless offers valuable insight for establishing such
\ell^{p}-\ell^{p^{\prime}}
decay estimates for arbitrary
a\in\mathbb{R}
.
Actually, using the idea developed in
[
28
]
, one can also prove that (
1.7
) is true for
p=1
and
a\neq 0
.

1.3.
The outline of the proof

In this subsection, we are devoted to presenting the key ideas of the proof for the above theorems. In view of the relation
W_{-}f=\overline{W_{+}\overline{f}}
, it suffices to analyze
W_{+}
alone. Starting from Stone’s formula, we obtain the representation for
W_{+}
:

\displaystyle W_{+}=I-\frac{2}{\pi i}\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})V(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})d\mu.

(1.8)

The first problem arisen here is to establish the existence of boundary values
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
. It is well-known that the limiting absorption principle (LAP) generally states that the resolvent
(H-z)^{-1}
may converge in a suitable way as
z
approaches spectrum points, which plays a fundamental role in spectral and scattering theory. For instance, see Agmon’s work
[
1
]
for the Schrödinger operator
-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}+V
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In the discrete setting, the LAP for discrete Schrödinger operators
-\Delta_{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}+V
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
has been extensively studied (cf.
[
18
,
53
,
8
,
9
,
36
,
37
,
29
,
41
,
48
]
and references therein).

However, to the best of our knowledge, it seems that LAP is open for higher-order Schrödinger operators on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. In our recent work
[
28
, Section 2]
, we addressed this issue for the fourth-order operator
H=\Delta^{2}+V
defined in (
1.2
) by employing commutator estimates and Mourre theory (cf.
[
32
,
42
,
43
]
). Specifically, under appropriate conditions on
V
, we proved that
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
for
H
exist as elements of some weighted spaces
\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))
(see Lemma
2.2
).

Throughout the paper, we denote by
K
the operator with kernel
K(n,m)
, i.e.,

(Kf)(n):=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}K(n,m)f(m).

We can obtain the explicit expression for the kernel of free resolvent
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
(see Lemma
2.1
):

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\Big(\frac{\pm ie^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-m|}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}-\frac{e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}\Big),

(1.9)

where
\theta_{+}:=\theta_{+}(\mu)
satisfies
2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=\mu^{2}
with
\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
and
b(\mu)={\rm ln}\big(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-\mu(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)
.
We note that the resolvent
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
exhibits singular behavior with order
O(\mu^{-3})
near
\mu=0
and
O((2-\mu)^{-1/2})
near
\mu=2
. Based on this observation, given a sufficiently small fixed positive constant
0<\mu_{0}\ll 1
, we consider the partition of unity:

\chi_{1}(\mu)+\chi_{2}(\mu)+\chi_{3}(\mu)=1,

where
\chi_{1}(\mu)\in C^{\infty}_{0}([0,\mu_{0}])
,
\chi_{2}(\mu)\in C^{\infty}_{0}([\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}])
and
\chi_{3}(\mu)\in C^{\infty}_{0}([2-\mu_{0},2])
. Correspondingly,

\displaystyle W_{+}=I-\frac{2}{\pi i}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{j}(\mu)R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})V(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})d\mu:=I-\frac{2}{\pi i}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{K}_{j}.

(1.10)

Therefore, the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of
W_{+}
reduces to establishing the boundedness of each part
\mathcal{K}_{j}
. Among these parts, the intermediate energy part
\mathcal{K}_{2}
is easier to handle since the resolvent does not have singularity in the interval
[\mu_{0},2-\mu_{0}]
. Indeed, as shown in Section
4
,
\mathcal{K}_{2}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Consequently, much effort in this article is devoted to handling the low energy part
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and the high energy part
\mathcal{K}_{3}
, which constitutes the main challenge of this paper.

To overcome this, a key point involves analyzing the asymptotic behaviors of
R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
. To this end, we introduce

M(\mu)=U+vR^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})v,\quad\mu\in(0,2),\quad U={\rm sign}\left(V(n)\right),\quad v(n)=\sqrt{|V(n)|}.

As established in Lemma
2.3
,
M(\mu)
is invertible on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and its inverse
M^{-1}(\mu)
satisfies

R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})V=R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vM^{-1}(\mu)v.

This allows us to reformulate
\mathcal{K}_{j}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{j}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{j}(\mu)R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vM^{-1}(\mu)v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})d\mu,\quad j=1,3.

(1.11)

Hence, the asymptotic expansions of
M^{-1}(\mu)
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
are crucial. These expansions were derived in our recent work
[
28
, Theorem 1.8]
and will be restated in Lemma
2.8
. The basic idea behind the expansions of
M^{-1}(\mu)
is the Neumann expansion, which in turn depends on the expansion of
R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})
. In this respect, Jensen and Kato initiated their seminal work in
[
31
]
for Schrödinger operator
-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}+V
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Since then, the method has been widely applied (cf.
[
33
,
55
]
).
When considering the discrete bi-Laplacian
\Delta^{2}
on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
, we will face two distinct difficulties.
Firstly, compared with Laplacian
-\Delta
on
\mathbb{Z}
, the threshold
0
now is a
degenerate critical value
(i.e.,
\mathcal{M}(0)=\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime}}(0)=\mathcal{M}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}(0)=0
, where the symbol
\mathcal{M}(x)=(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}
is defined in (
2.2
)). This degeneracy leads to additional steps to expand the
M^{-1}(\mu)
. Secondly, in contrast to the continuous analogue
[
55
]
, we encounter another threshold 16 (i.e., corresponding to
\mu=2
).

With these expansions in hand, the next crucial step is to utilize them to establish the desired boundedness of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{3}
. For simplicity,
we consider the representative case where both
0
and
16
are regular points of the operator
H
. The subsequent analysis will be divided into two parts.

1.3.1.
On the
\ell^{p}
boundedness

•

For the low energy part
{\bm{\mathcal{K}_{1}}}.
We first note that in expression (
1.9
),
\theta_{+}
and
b(\mu)
exhibit

the following behaviors, respectively:

\theta_{+}=-\mu+o(\mu),\quad b(\mu)=-\mu+o(\mu),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}.

This shows that the kernel
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)
closely resembles its continuous counterpart (cf.
[
55
]
):

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},x,y)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\big(\pm ie^{\pm i\mu|x-y|}-e^{-\mu|x-y|}\big),\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

This observation inspires us that we may also try to combine the Taylor expansion of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)
and the orthogonality

\displaystyle Qv=0=S_{0}(v_{k}),\quad\left<Qf,v\right>=0=\left<S_{0}f,v_{k}\right>,\quad v_{k}(n)=n^{k}v(n),\quad k=0,1,

of the orthogonal projection operators
Q,S_{0}
in the expansion (
(i)
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}M^{-1}(\mu)&=S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}+\mu QA_{1}Q+\mu^{2}(QA^{0}_{21}Q+S_{0}A^{0}_{22}+A^{0}_{23}S_{0})\\
&\quad+\mu^{3}(QA^{0}_{31}+A^{0}_{32}Q)+\mu^{3}P_{1}+\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu)\end{split}

(1.12)

to eliminate the singularity of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
near
\mu=0
as its continuous analogue
[
44
, Lemma 2.5]
. However, unlike the perfect form in the continuous case, the more complex structure of the kernel
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)
introduces significant technical challenges. To address this issue, we establish a modified cancelation Lemma
3.2
to derive

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQ=O(\mu^{-2}),\ R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{0}=O(\mu^{-1}),\ QvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(\mu^{-2}),\ S_{0}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(\mu^{-1}).

By virtue of this property, we can classify the operators in (
1.12
) above into the following two groups according to the order of
K_{A}(n,m)
with regard to
\mu
:

O(\mu):S_{0}A_{0}S_{0},\mu^{2}QA^{0}_{21}Q,\mu^{2}S_{0}A^{0}_{22},\mu^{2}A^{0}_{23}S_{0},\mu^{3}QA^{0}_{31},\mu^{3}A^{0}_{32}Q,\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu),\quad O(1):\mu QA_{1}Q,\mu^{3}P_{1},

where

K_{A}(n,m)=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{1}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAv(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

(1.13)

Substituting (
1.12
) into (
1.11
) for
j=1
, we can further express
\mathcal{K}_{1}
as the sum

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{1}=\sum\limits_{A\in O(\mu)}K_{A}+\sum\limits_{A\in O(1)}K_{A}.

For the operators in class
O(\mu)
, we can prove that
K_{A}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
. We shall explain this for
A=S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}
as a model case. In this case, by means of Lemma
3.2

and the variable substitution:

{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\Longrightarrow\ \frac{d\mu}{d\theta_{+}}=-\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}},\quad\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 0\ {\rm and}\ \theta_{+}\rightarrow-\pi\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 2,

(1.14)

we can rewrite (
1.13
) as a linear combination of the following functions:

\displaystyle K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}g_{1}(\theta_{+})\chi_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))L^{\pm,1}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}g_{2}(\mu)\chi_{1}(\mu)L^{\pm,2}_{0}(\mu,n,m)d\mu,

\displaystyle K^{\pm,3}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|n|+|m|)}g_{3}(\theta_{+}){\chi}_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))L^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+},

where
g_{j}
and
L^{\pm,j}_{0}
satisfy the following properties for any
k=0,1,2
, respectively:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}g^{(k)}_{j}(\theta_{+})\quad{\rm and}\quad\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0}g^{(k)}_{2}(\mu)\quad{\rm exist},

(1.15)

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j\in\{1,3\}}\sup\limits_{\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)}\big|(\partial^{k}_{\theta_{+}}

\displaystyle L^{\pm,j}_{0})(\theta_{+},n,m)\big|+\sup\limits_{\mu\in(0,\mu_{0}]}\big|e^{b(\mu)|n|}(\partial^{k}_{\mu}L^{\pm,2}_{0})(\mu,n,m)\big|\lesssim\|\left<\cdot\right>^{2k+4}V(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}},

uniformly in
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Moreover, we have
\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}g_{j}(\theta_{+})=0
and
\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0}g_{2}(\mu)=0
. This property, combined with

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\big|b^{\prime}(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta^{\prime}_{+}(\mu)|m|\big|^{2}}\lesssim(|n|+|m|)^{-2},\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ (n,m)\neq(0,0)\ {\rm and}\ \mu\in(0,2),

enables us to apply integration by parts twice to
K^{\pm,j}_{0}(n,m)
obtaining

|K^{\pm,j}_{0}(n,m)|\lesssim\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2},\quad j=1,2,3.

Then the
\ell^{p}
boundedness for
1\leq p\leq\infty
is derived by the Shur test Lemma
3.4
.

In the preceding analysis, we notice that a crucial point is that the limits of
g_{j}
vanish as
\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0
and
\mu\rightarrow 0
. However, for operators in class
O(1)
, which lack this vanishing property, the singular terms
O(\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-1})
will emerge. To solve this problem, we shall appeal to the theory of the discrete singular integrals on the lattice, see Appendix
A
.

Specifically, we consider
A=\mu^{3}P_{1}
as a model case of the operator class
O(1)
. In this case, an analogous argument as above yields that (
1.13
) can be written as a linear combination of these functions:

\displaystyle K^{\pm,1}_{P_{1}}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}h_{1}(\theta_{+}){\chi}_{1}(\mu(\theta_{+}))L^{\pm,1}_{P_{1}}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+},

\displaystyle K^{\pm,2}_{P_{1}}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}h_{2}(\mu){\chi}_{1}(\mu)L^{\pm,2}_{P_{1}}(\mu,n,m)d\mu,

where
h_{j}
and
L^{\pm,j}_{P_{1}}
satisfy the similar property as in (
1.15
). Applying integration by parts twice to
K^{\pm,j}_{P_{1}}(n,m)
, we find that (
1.13
) equals

\frac{i-1}{8}\Big(k^{+}_{1}(n,m)+k^{-}_{1}(n,m)+k^{+}_{2}(n,m)+k^{-}_{2}(n,m)\Big)+O(\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2}),

where

k^{\pm}_{1}(n,m)=\frac{\phi(||n|\pm|m||^{2})}{|n|\pm|m|},\quad k^{\pm}_{2}(n,m)=\frac{\phi(||n|-|m||^{2})}{|n|\pm i|m|}

with
\phi(s)
being smooth cut-off functions supported in
\{s:|s|\geq 1\}
.
In view that the integral operator
T_{k^{\pm}_{\ell}}
associated with the kernel
k^{\pm}_{\ell}(x,y)
in the continuous setting is not a Calderón-Zygmund operator, we cannot directly apply Theorem
A.1
. To overcome this, we utilize the following relation:

({k}^{\pm}_{1}f)(n)=\big[(\chi_{+}\widetilde{k}_{1}\chi_{\mp}-\chi_{-}\widetilde{k}_{1}\chi_{\pm})(1+\tau)f\big](n),\ ({k}^{\pm}_{2}f)(n)=\big[(\chi_{+}\widetilde{k}^{\pm}_{2}\chi_{+}-\chi_{-}\widetilde{k}^{\pm}_{2}\chi_{-})(1+\tau)f\big](n),

where
\chi_{\pm}=\chi_{\mathbb{Z}^{\pm}}
is the characteristic function on
\mathbb{Z}^{\pm}:=\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:\pm m>0\}
,
(\tau f)(n)=f(-n)
and

\widetilde{k}_{1}(n,m)=\frac{\phi(|n-m|^{2})}{n-m},\quad\widetilde{k}^{\pm}_{2}(n,m)=\frac{\phi(|n-m|^{2})}{n\pm im}.

This relation allows us to reduce the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of
k^{\pm}_{1}
to
\widetilde{k}_{1}
, and
k^{\pm}_{2}
to
\widetilde{k}^{\pm}_{2}
, both of which are
\ell^{p}
bounded for
1<p<\infty
,
since their continuous analogues
T_{\widetilde{k}_{1}}
and
T_{\widetilde{k}^{\pm}_{2}}
are Calderón-Zygmund operators (for further details, we refer to
[
44
, Lemma 3.3]
).

•

For the high energy part
{\bm{\mathcal{K}_{3}}}.
In this case,
the first distinction from
\mathcal{K}_{1}
is that it is not

straightforward to utilize the orthogonality

\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}=0=\big<\widetilde{Q}f,\tilde{v}\big>

of the projection operator
\widetilde{Q}
in the expansion (
2.11
)

\displaystyle M^{-1}(\mu)=\widetilde{Q}B_{0}\widetilde{Q}+(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\widetilde{Q}B^{0}_{11}+B^{0}_{12}\widetilde{Q})+(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}+(2-\mu)B^{0}_{21}+\Gamma^{0}_{\frac{3}{2}}(2-\mu)

to eliminate the singularity at
\mu=2
, where
\tilde{v}(n)=(Jv)(n):=(-1)^{n}v(n)
. To this end, we will make use of the unitary transform
J
above, which satisfies

JR^{\pm}_{-\Delta}(\mu^{2})J=-R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2}),\quad\mu\in(0,2).

This formula together with
J^{2}=I
allows us to rewrite

R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vBv(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})=\frac{1}{4\mu^{4}}J\big(R^{-}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})+JR_{-\Delta}(-\mu^{2})J\big)\tilde{v}B\tilde{v}(R^{-}_{-\Delta}-R^{+}_{-\Delta})(4-\mu^{2})J.

(1.16)

Such form motivates us to combine the orthogonality of
\widetilde{Q}
above and the Taylor expansion of

\displaystyle R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2},n,m)=\frac{\pm ie^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n-m|}}{2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}=\frac{\pm i}{2{\rm sin}\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\Big(e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n|}\mp i\tilde{\theta}_{+}m\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}|n-\rho m|}d\rho\Big)

deriving

R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})\tilde{v}\widetilde{Q}=O(1),\quad\widetilde{Q}\tilde{v}R^{\mp}_{-\Delta}(4-\mu^{2})=O(1),

where
\tilde{\theta}_{+}:=\tilde{\theta}_{+}(\mu)
satisfies
{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-1
with
\tilde{\theta}_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
.
Similarly, we can express
\mathcal{K}_{3}
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{3}=\sum\limits_{B\in O(1)}K_{B}+\sum\limits_{B\in O((2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}})}K_{B}.

Here

O(1):\widetilde{Q}B_{0}\widetilde{Q},(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{Q}B^{0}_{11},(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{0}_{12}\widetilde{Q},(2-\mu)B^{0}_{21},\Gamma^{0}_{\frac{3}{2}}(2-\mu),\quad O((2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}):(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}

and

K_{B}(n,m)=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{3}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vBv(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

(1.17)

We will prove that
K_{B}
is
\ell^{p}
bounded for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
for the operators in the class
O(1)
and
\ell^{p}
bounded for
1<p<\infty
for the operators in the class
O((2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}})
.

For further explanation, we consider
B=\widetilde{Q}B_{0}\widetilde{Q}
as a model case of the class
O(1)
. In this case, by virtue of (
5.2
) and the following variable substitution:

\displaystyle{\rm cos}\tilde{\theta}_{+}=\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-1\Longrightarrow\ \frac{d\mu}{d\tilde{\theta}_{+}}=\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}},\quad\tilde{\theta}_{+}\rightarrow-\pi\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 0\ {\rm and}\ \tilde{\theta}_{+}\rightarrow 0\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 2,

(1.18)

we can express (
1.17
) as a linear combination of the following functions:

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{n+m}\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{i\tilde{\theta}_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}\tilde{g}_{1}(\tilde{\theta}_{+}){\chi}_{3}(\mu(\tilde{\theta}_{+}))\widetilde{L}^{\pm,1}_{0}(\tilde{\theta}_{+},n,m)d\tilde{\theta}_{+},

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{\pm i\tilde{\theta}_{+}(|m|\pm i|n|)}\tilde{g}_{2}(\tilde{\theta}_{+}){\chi}_{3}(\mu(\tilde{\theta}_{+}))\widetilde{L}^{\pm,2}_{0}(\mu(\tilde{\theta}_{+}),n,m)d\tilde{\theta}_{+},

where
\tilde{g}_{j}(\tilde{\theta}_{+})
satisfies the similar property (
1.15
) and

\sup\limits_{\tilde{\theta}_{+}\in(-\pi,0)}\big|(\partial^{k}_{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\widetilde{L}^{\pm,1}_{0})(\tilde{\theta}_{+},n,m)\big|+\sup\limits_{\tilde{\theta}_{+}\in[\gamma_{1},0)}\big|(\partial^{k}_{\tilde{\theta}_{+}}\widetilde{L}^{\pm,2}_{0})(\mu(\tilde{\theta}_{+}),n,m)\big|\lesssim\|\left<\cdot\right>^{2k+2}V(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}},\ k=0,1,2

uniformly in
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\gamma_{1}\in(-\pi,0)
satisfies
{\rm cos}\gamma_{1}=\frac{(2-\mu_{0})^{2}}{2}-1
. Thanks to the substitution (
1.18
) above, which contributes a factor of
(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, this allows that the limits of
\tilde{g}_{j}(\tilde{\theta}_{+})
vanish as
\tilde{\theta}_{+}\rightarrow 0
. This constitutes another difference from
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, where the variable substitution (
1.14
) does not alter the singularity near
\mu=0
. Using the argument analogous to
K^{\pm,j}_{0}
above, we can also derive

|\widetilde{K}^{\pm,j}_{0}(n,m)|\lesssim\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2},\quad j=1,2,

and so the desired result is obtained. For the operator
(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}
, combining the arguments for
K_{B}
with
{\widetilde{Q}B_{0}\widetilde{Q}}
and
K_{A}
with
A=\mu^{3}P_{1}
above, we can demonstrate that (
1.17
) equals

\displaystyle\frac{(-1)^{n+m}}{4}\big(k^{+}_{1}(n,m)+k^{-}_{1}(n,m)\big)+O(\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2}),

which is
\ell^{p}
bounded for
1<p<\infty
.

In summary, we establish
W_{+}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1<p<\infty
.

1.3.2.
Counterexamples of
\ell^{1}
and
\ell^{\infty}
boundedness

As demonstrated above, the operators in
O(\mu)
for
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and those in
O(1)
for
\mathcal{K}_{3}
are always
\ell^{p}
bounded for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Consequently, the boundedness of
W_{+}
at the endpoints
p=1,\infty
reduces
to analyzing the operators
K_{1}
,
K_{P_{1}}
and
K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}
, where

K_{1}=K_{A}~(A=\mu QA_{1}Q),\quad K_{P_{1}}=K_{A}~(A=\mu^{3}P_{1}),\quad K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}=K_{B}~(B=(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}).

These three operators may fail to be bounded on both
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
. While this observation is not sufficient to disprove such boundedness of
W_{+}
, we can examine the behavior by taking the characteristic function
f_{N}(n):=\chi_{[-N,N]}(n)
on the interval
[-N,N]
with
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
as test function. Specifically, we can show

•

|(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}})f_{N}(N+2)|\rightarrow+\infty,N\rightarrow+\infty
and
(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}})f_{1}\notin\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
,

•

\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|K_{1}f_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty
and
K_{1}f_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

This gives that
W_{+}\notin\mathbb{B}(\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}))\cup\mathbb{B}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}))
.

1.4.
Organizations of the paper

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows. Section
2
presents preliminary materials, including the limiting absorption principle and asymptotic expansions of
M^{-1}(\mu)
. Sections
3
\sim
5
are devoted to the proof of Theorem
1.3
, while Section
6
contains the proof of Theorem
1.4
. In Section
7
, we will apply the previously established
\ell^{p}
-boundedness of wave operators to derive decay estimates for solutions of the discrete beam equation (
1.5
). Finally, Appendix
A
provides a review of discrete Calderón-Zygmund operators on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

2.
Preliminaries

This section is devoted to establishing the limiting absorption principle for the operator
H
and investigating the asymptotic behaviors of
R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
.

2.1.
Limiting absorption principle

We begin by recalling some basics of the resolvents. From the definition of Laplacian
\Delta
on
\mathbb{Z}
in (
1.1
), the bi-Laplacian
\Delta^{2}
on
\mathbb{Z}
is given by

(\Delta^{2}\phi)(n)=(\Delta(\Delta\phi))(n)=\phi(n+2)-4\phi(n+1)+6\phi(n)-4\phi(n-1)+\phi(n-2).

Consider the Fourier transform
\mathcal{F}
:
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow L^{2}(\mathbb{T}),\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/2\pi\mathbb{Z}=[-\pi,\pi]
, defined by

(\mathcal{F}\phi)(x):=(2\pi)^{-{\frac{1}{2}}}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{-inx}\phi(n),\quad\forall\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(2.1)

Under this transform, we have

(\mathcal{F}\Delta^{2}\phi)(x)=(2-2{\rm cos}x)^{2}(\mathcal{F}\phi)(x):=\mathcal{M}(x)(\mathcal{F}\phi)(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{T}=[-\pi,\pi],

(2.2)

which implies that the spectrum of
\Delta^{2}
is purely absolutely continuous and equals
[0,16]
. Let

\quad R_{0}(z):=(\Delta^{2}-z)^{-1},\quad R_{V}(z):=(H-z)^{-1},\quad z\in\rho(H)~{\rm(the\ resolvent\ set\ of}\ H{\rm)}

be the resolvents of
\Delta^{2}
and
H
, respectively and define their boundary values on
(0,16)
via

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda):=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{0}(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon),\quad R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda):=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{V}(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon),\quad\lambda\in(0,16).

The existence of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\lambda)
in
\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
follows from the resolvent decomposition

R_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{z}}\left(R_{-\Delta}(\sqrt{z})-R_{-\Delta}(-\sqrt{z})\right),\quad\sqrt{z}=\sqrt{|z|}e^{i\frac{argz}{2}},\ 0<argz<2\pi,

and the known limiting absorption principle for
-\Delta
(cf.
[
36
]
)

R^{\pm}_{-\Delta}(\mu):=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R_{-\Delta}(\mu\pm i\varepsilon),\quad\mu\in(0,4),

which exists in the operator norm of
\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))
for
s>\frac{1}{2}
, where
R_{-\Delta}(\omega)=(-\Delta-\omega)^{-1}
is the resolvent of
-\Delta
.

Moreover, using the kernel of
R_{-\Delta}(\omega)
(cf.
[
36
]
):

R_{-\Delta}(\omega,n,m)=\frac{-ie^{-i\theta(\omega)|n-m|}}{2{\rm sin}\theta(\omega)},\quad n,m\in\mathbb{Z},

(2.3)

where
\theta(\omega)
is the solution to the equation
2-2{\rm cos}\theta=\omega

in the domain

\mathcal{D}:=\left\{\theta(\omega)=a+ib:-\pi\leq a\leq\pi,b<0\right\},

we can derive explicit expression for the integral kernel of the free resolvent
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
as follows.

Lemma 2.1
.

(
[
28
, Lemma 2.3]
)

For
\mu\in(0,2)
, the kernel of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
is given by

\displaystyle R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\Big(\pm ia_{1}(\mu)e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-m|}+a_{2}(\mu)e^{b(\mu)|n-m|}\Big),

(2.4)

where
\theta_{+}:=\theta_{+}(\mu)
satisfies
2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=\mu^{2}
with
\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)
and

a_{1}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}},\quad a_{2}(\mu)=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}},\quad b(\mu)={\rm ln}\big(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-\mu(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big).

(2.5)

As shown above, the resolvent
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
exhibits singular behavior with order
O(\mu^{-3})
near
\mu=0
and
O((2-\mu)^{-1/2})
near
\mu=2
. Indeed, more precise asymptotic expansions of
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})
near these critical points in suitable weighted space
\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))
can be found in
[
28
, Lemma 5.4]
.

For the perturbed operator
H=\Delta^{2}+V
, we can derive the following LAP.

Lemma 2.2
.

(
[
28
, Theorem 2.4]
)

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>1
and
\mathcal{I}=(0,16)
. Denote by
[\beta]
the biggest integer no more than
\beta
. Then the following statements hold.

(i)

The point spectrum
\sigma_{p}(H)\cap\mathcal{I}
is discrete, with each eigenvalue has a finite multiplicity, and the singular continuous spectrum
\sigma_{sc}(H)=\varnothing
.

(ii)

Let
j\in\left\{0,\cdots,[\beta]-1\right\}
and
j+\frac{1}{2}<s\leq[\beta]
, then the following norm limits

\frac{d^{j}}{d\lambda^{j}}(R^{\pm}_{V}(\lambda))=\lim\limits_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}R^{(j)}_{V}(\lambda\pm i\varepsilon)\quad{\rm in}\quad\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))

are norm continuous from
\mathcal{I}\setminus\sigma_{p}(H)
to
\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))
, where
R^{(j)}_{V}(z)
denotes the jth derivative of
R_{V}(z)
.

We remark that the derivation of this LAP relies on the commutator estimates and Mourre theory. The upper bound of
s
is closely related to the regularity of
H
. For more details, see
[
28
, Section 2 and Appendix A]
.

Throughout the paper, we always assume that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in
\mathcal{I}
. As a consequence of this lemma,
R^{\pm}_{V}(\mu^{4})
exists in
\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))
for
\frac{1}{2}<s\leq[\beta]
and all
\mu\in(0,2)
.

2.2.
Asymptotic expansions of
R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})

In this subsection, we further investigate the asymptotic behaviors of
R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
. For this purpose, we introduce

M(\mu)=U+vR^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})v,\quad\mu\in(0,2),\quad U={\rm sign}\left(V(n)\right),\quad v(n)=\sqrt{|V(n)|},

and denote the inverse by
M^{-1}(\mu)
as long as it exists. Indeed, building upon Lemma
2.2
, we can establish such invertibility. Moreover, it allows us to reduce the asymptotic analysis of
R_{V}^{+}(\mu^{4})
to studying the behavior of
M^{-1}(\mu)
near
\mu=0
and
\mu=2
. Specifically, we have:

Lemma 2.3
.

(
[
28
, Corollary 2.5]
)

Let
H,V
be as in Lemma
2.2
. For any
\mu\in(0,2)
,
M(\mu)
is invertible on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and satisfies the relation below in
\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2,s}(\mathbb{Z}),\ell^{2,-s}(\mathbb{Z}))
for
\frac{1}{2}<s\leq[\beta]
:

R^{+}_{V}(\mu^{4})V=R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vM^{-1}(\mu)v.

(2.6)

Prior to presenting the asymptotic expansions of
M^{-1}(\mu)
, we first recall another characterization of zero and sixteen resonances of
H
established in
[
28
, Remark 5.3]
, which provides a direct approach to compute
M^{-1}(\mu)
via Von-Neumann series expansion.
Let

\left<f,g\right>:=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}f(m)\overline{g(m)},\quad f,g\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

For simplicity, we denote the kernel of the operator
\mathcal{T}
restricted to the space
X
by
{\rm Ker}\mathcal{T}\big|_{X}=\{f\in X:\mathcal{T}f=0\}
.

Definition 2.4
.

(
Zero resonances
)
Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
and
G_{j}

(
j=-1,0,1,3
)
be the integral operator with kernel
G_{j}(n,m)
:

\displaystyle G_{-1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{8}-\frac{1}{2}|n-m|^{2},\quad G_{0}(n,m)=\frac{1}{12}\left(|n-m|^{3}-|n-m|\right),

\displaystyle G_{1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3}|n-m|^{4}-\frac{5}{6}|n-m|^{2}+\frac{3}{16},

\displaystyle G_{3}(n,m)

\displaystyle=|n-m|^{6}-\frac{35}{4}|n-m|^{4}+\frac{259}{16}|n-m|^{2}-\frac{225}{64}.

Let
I
be the identity operator and define

P:=\left\|V\right\|^{-1}_{\ell^{1}}\left<\cdot,v\right>v,\quad Q:=I-P,\quad T:=U+vG_{0}v.

Let
S_{0}
be the orthogonal projection onto the kernel space
{\rm Ker}QvG_{-1}vQ\big|_{Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}

and denote by
D_{0}
the inverse of
QvG_{-1}vQ+S_{0}
on
Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. We say that

(i)

{\bm{0}}
is
a regular point of

{\bm{H}}
if
T_{0}:=S_{0}TS_{0}:S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
is invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii)

Assume that
T_{0}
is not invertible on
S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let
S_{1}
be the orthogonal projection onto the kernel space
{\rm Ker}T_{0}\big|_{S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
.
We say that
{\bm{0}}
is
a first kind resonance of

{\bm{H}}
if

\displaystyle T_{1}=S_{1}vG_{1}vS_{1}+\frac{8}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}S_{1}vG_{-1}vPvG_{-1}vS_{1}+64S_{1}TD_{0}TS_{1}

is invertible on
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(iii)

Assume that
T_{1}
is not invertible on
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let
S_{2}
be the orthogonal projection onto the kernel space
{\rm Ker}T_{1}\big|_{S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
and
denote by
D_{2}
the inverse of
T_{1}+S_{2}
on
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Then we say that
{\bm{0}}
is
a second kind resonance of

{\bm{H}}
if

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6!}\Big(S_{2}vG_{3}vS_{2}-\frac{8\cdot 6!}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}S_{2}{T}^{2}S_{2}-\frac{6!}{64}S_{2}vG_{1}vD_{0}vG_{1}vS_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{64}{\|V\|^{2}_{\ell^{1}}}\big(S_{2}TvG_{-1}vD_{0}-\frac{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}{8}S_{2}vG_{1}vD_{0}TD_{0}\big)D_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times\big(D_{0}vG_{-1}vTS_{2}-\frac{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}{8}D_{0}TD_{0}vG_{1}vS_{2}\big)

is invertible on
S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Definition 2.5
.

(
Sixteen resonances
)

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
and
\widetilde{G}_{j}~(j=0,1,2)
be the integral operator with kernel
\widetilde{G}_{j}(n,m)
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{32\sqrt{2}}\big(2\sqrt{2}|n-m|-{(2\sqrt{2}-3)}^{|n-m|}\big),\quad\widetilde{G}_{1}(n,m)=2|n-m|^{2}-\frac{13}{8},

\displaystyle\widetilde{G}_{2}(n,m)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{24}|n-m|^{3}+\frac{5}{48}|n-m|-\big(2\sqrt{2}-3\big)^{|n-m|}\Big(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}|n-m|-\frac{1}{8}+\frac{15}{256\sqrt{2}}\Big).

Define

\widetilde{P}:=\left\|V\right\|^{-1}_{\ell^{1}}\left<\cdot,\tilde{v}\right>\tilde{v},\quad\widetilde{Q}:=I-\widetilde{P},\quad\widetilde{T}:=U+\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v},\quad\tilde{v}(n)=(Jv)(n):=(-1)^{n}v(n).

(2.7)

(i)

{\bm{16}}
is
a regular point of

{\bm{H}}
if
\widetilde{T}_{0}:=\widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}\widetilde{Q}
is invertible on
\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(ii)

Assume that
\widetilde{T}_{0}
is not invertible on
\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\widetilde{S}_{0}
be the orthogonal projection onto the kernel space
{\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{0}\big|_{\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
. We say that

{\bm{16}}
is
a resonance of

{\bm{H}}
if

\widetilde{T}_{1}=\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{1}\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{0}+\frac{32}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}\widetilde{S}_{0}\widetilde{T}^{2}\widetilde{S}_{0}\ \ is\ \ invertible\ on\ \ \widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

(iii)

Assume that
\widetilde{T}_{1}
is not invertible on
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let
\widetilde{S}_{1}
be the orthogonal projection onto the kernel space
{\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{1}\big|_{\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}
. We say that

{\bm{16}}
is
an eigenvalue of

{\bm{H}}
if

\widetilde{T}_{2}=\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}\widetilde{G}_{2}\tilde{v}\widetilde{S}_{1}
is invertible on
\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Remark 2.6
.

(1) We point out that
G_{0},J\widetilde{G}_{0}J
actually are the fundamental solutions of
\Delta^{2}
and
\Delta^{2}-16
, respectively, i.e.,
\Delta^{2}G_{0}=\delta
and
(\Delta^{2}-16)J\widetilde{G}_{0}J=\delta
.

(2) When
\beta>9
, we can obtain concise expressions of the kernel subspaces, i.e., orthogonal projection spaces
S_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\widetilde{S}_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
above.

•

Orthogonal projection spaces
S_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

\displaystyle S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle={\rm Ker}QvG_{-1}vQ\big|_{Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1\big\},\quad v_{k}(n)=n^{k}v(n),

\displaystyle S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle={\rm Ker}T_{0}\big|_{S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,\ S_{0}Tf=0\big\},

\displaystyle S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle={\rm Ker}T_{1}\big|_{S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,2,\ QTf=0\big\},

\displaystyle S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle:={\rm Ker}T_{2}\big|_{S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,v_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,2,3,\ Tf=0\big\}.

•

Orthogonal projection spaces
\widetilde{S}_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle={\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{0}\big|_{\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,\tilde{v}\right>=0,\ \widetilde{Q}\widetilde{T}f=0\big\},\quad\tilde{v}_{k}=Jv_{k},

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle={\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{1}\big|_{\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,\tilde{v}_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,\ \widetilde{T}_{0}f=0\big\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle:={\rm Ker}\widetilde{T}_{2}\big|_{\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})}=\big\{f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}):\left<f,\tilde{v}_{k}\right>=0,\ k=0,1,\ \widetilde{T}_{2}f=0\big\}.

We note that these spaces have the following inclusion relations:

\displaystyle S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq S_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq Q\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\subseteq\widetilde{Q}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

Particularly, we can show that
S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
and
\widetilde{S}_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
, which completes the entire inversion process of
M^{-1}(\mu)
. For a detailed proof of these facts, we refer to
[
28
, Lemma 5.2]
.

In other word, Definitions
2.4
\sim
2.5
can be simply expressed as:

(i)

0
is a regular point of
H
if and only if
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(ii)

0
is a first kind resonance of
H
if and only if
S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
and
S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(iii)

0
is a second kind resonance of
H
if and only if
S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
.

(iv)

16
is a regular point of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(v)

16
is a resonance of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
and
\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})=\{0\}
.

(vi)

16
is an eigenvalue of
H
if and only if
\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\{0\}
.

(3) Moreover, we remark that these orthogonal projection spaces
S_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
(resp.
\widetilde{S}_{j}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
) are intimately linked to the solutions of difference equation
H\phi=0
(resp.
H\phi=16\phi
) in suitable weighted space
W_{\sigma}(\mathbb{Z})
. More precisely, we have

Lemma 2.7
.

(
[
28
, Lemma 6.1]
)

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
on
\mathbb{Z}
and
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
with
\beta>9
, then

(i)

f\in S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=0
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and
\phi(n)=-(G_{0}vf)(n)+c_{1}n+c_{2}
,
where

c_{1}=\frac{\left<Tf,v^{\prime}\right>}{\|v^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\ell^{2}}},\quad c_{2}=\frac{\left<Tf,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}c_{1},\quad v^{\prime}=Q(v_{1})=v_{1}-\frac{\left<v_{1},v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}v.

(ii)

f\in S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=0
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and

\phi=-G_{0}vf+\frac{\left<Tf,v\right>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}.

(iii)

f\in S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=0
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and
\phi=-G_{0}vf
.

(iv)

f\in\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=16\phi
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and

\phi=-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f+J\frac{\big<\widetilde{T}f,\tilde{v}\big>}{\|V\|_{\ell^{1}}}.

(v)

f\in\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})\Longleftrightarrow\exists\ \phi\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
H\phi=16\phi
. Moreover,
f=Uv\phi
and
\phi=-J\widetilde{G}_{0}\tilde{v}f
.

This lemma indicates that

\displaystyle S_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=0\ {\rm has\ only\ zero\ solution\ in\ }W_{3/2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle S_{2}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=0\ {\rm has\ only\ zero\ solution\ in\ }W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle S_{3}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=0\ {\rm has\ only\ zero\ solution\ in\ }\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{0}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=16\phi\ {\rm has\ only\ zero\ solution\ in\ }W_{1/2}(\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{S}_{1}\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\{0\}\Leftrightarrow H\phi=16\phi\ {\rm has\ only\ zero\ solution\ in\ }\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}).

We now give the asymptotic expansions of
M^{-1}(\mu)
as follows. Let

P_{1}:=-\frac{2(1+i)}{\left\|V\right\|_{\ell^{1}}}P,\quad\widetilde{P}_{1}:=-\frac{32i}{\left\|V\right\|_{\ell^{1}}}\widetilde{P}.

We say that an integral operator
K\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z}))
is absolutely bounded if its associated absolute value integral operator
|K|
, defined by the kernel
|K(n,m)|
, is also bounded on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Lemma 2.8
.

(
[
28
, Theorem 1.8]
)

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Then we have the following asymptotic expansions on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
for
0<\mu<\mu_{0}
:

(i)

if
0
is a regular point of
H
and
\beta>15
, then

\displaystyle M^{-1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}+\mu QA_{1}Q+\mu^{2}(QA^{0}_{21}Q+S_{0}A^{0}_{22}+A^{0}_{23}S_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\mu^{3}(QA^{0}_{31}+A^{0}_{32}Q)+\mu^{3}P_{1}+\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu),

(2.8)

(ii)

if
0
is a first kind resonance of
H
and
\beta>19
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}M^{-1}(\mu)&=\mu^{-1}S_{1}A_{-1}S_{1}+\big(S_{0}A^{1}_{01}Q+QA^{1}_{02}S_{0}\big)+\mu\big(S_{0}A^{1}_{11}+A^{1}_{12}S_{0}+QA^{1}_{13}Q\big)\\
&\quad+\mu^{2}\big(QA^{1}_{21}+A^{1}_{22}Q\big)+\mu^{3}(QA^{1}_{31}+A^{1}_{32}Q)+\mu^{3}P_{1}+\mu^{3}A^{1}_{33}+\Gamma^{1}_{4}(\mu),\end{split}

(2.9)

(iii)

if
0
is a second kind resonance of
H
and
\beta>27
, then

\displaystyle M^{-1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{S_{2}A_{-3}S_{2}}{\mu^{3}}+\frac{S_{2}A_{-2,1}S_{0}+S_{0}A_{-2,2}S_{2}}{\mu^{2}}+\frac{S_{2}A_{-1,1}Q+QA_{-1,2}S_{2}+S_{0}A_{-1,3}S_{0}}{\mu}

\displaystyle\quad+\big(S_{2}A^{2}_{01}+A^{2}_{02}S_{2}+QA^{2}_{03}S_{0}+S_{0}A^{2}_{04}Q\big)+\mu\big(S_{0}A^{2}_{11}+A^{2}_{12}S_{0}+QA^{2}_{13}Q\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu^{2}\big(QA^{2}_{21}+A^{2}_{22}Q\big)+\mu^{3}(QA^{2}_{31}+A^{2}_{32}Q)+\mu^{3}P_{1}+\mu^{3}A^{2}_{33}+\Gamma^{2}_{4}(\mu),

(2.10)

(iv)

if
16
is a regular point of
H
and
\beta>9
, then

\displaystyle M^{-1}(2-\mu)=\widetilde{Q}B_{0}\widetilde{Q}+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(\widetilde{Q}B^{0}_{11}+B^{0}_{12}\widetilde{Q})+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}+\mu B^{0}_{21}+\Gamma^{0}_{\frac{3}{2}}(\mu),

(2.11)

(v)

if
16
is a resonance of
H
and
\beta>13
, then

\displaystyle M^{-1}(2-\mu)

\displaystyle=\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{S}_{0}B_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0}+\big(\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{1}_{01}+B^{1}_{02}\widetilde{S}_{0}+\widetilde{Q}B^{1}_{03}\widetilde{Q}\big)+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(\widetilde{Q}B^{1}_{11}+B^{1}_{12}\widetilde{Q})

\displaystyle\quad+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}+\mu B^{1}_{21}+\Gamma^{1}_{\frac{3}{2}}(\mu),

(2.12)

(vi)

if
16
is an eigenvalue of
H
and
\beta>17
, then

\displaystyle M^{-1}(2-\mu)

\displaystyle=\mu^{-1}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}+\mu^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\widetilde{S}_{0}B_{-1,1}\widetilde{Q}+\widetilde{Q}B_{-1,2}\widetilde{S}_{0}\big)+\big(\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{01}+B^{2}_{02}\widetilde{Q}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}(\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{11}+B^{2}_{12}\widetilde{Q})+\mu^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}+\mu B^{2}_{21}+\Gamma^{2}_{\frac{3}{2}}(\mu),

(2.13)

where
A_{0},A_{1},A_{-1},A_{-3},A^{i}_{jk},A_{j,k},B_{0},B_{-1},B_{-2},B^{i}_{jk},B_{j,k}
are
\mu
-independent bounded operators on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
and
\Gamma^{i}_{\ell}(\mu)
are
\mu
-dependent bounded operators on
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
such that all the operators appeared in the right hand sides of (
(i)
)
\sim
(
(vi)
) are absolutely bounded. Moreover,
\Gamma^{i}_{\ell}(\mu)
satisfies the following estimates:

\|\Gamma^{i}_{\ell}(\mu)\|_{\ell^{2}\rightarrow\ell^{2}}+\mu\big\|\partial_{\mu}(\Gamma^{i}_{\ell}(\mu))\big\|_{\ell^{2}\rightarrow\ell^{2}}\lesssim\mu^{\ell}.

(2.14)

Remark 2.9
.

We note that in
[
28
, Theorem 1.8]
, the precise information of the
\mu^{3}
term in
M^{-1}(\mu)
near
\mu=0
is not required. However, for our analysis of
\ell^{p}
boundedness, this detailed information becomes essential and can be extracted from the proof given in
[
28
, Section 5]
. Furthermore, we require more terms in the expansion of
M^{-1}(\mu)
around
\mu=2
, which can also be obtained by following the analogous arguments in
[
28
, Section 5]
.

3.
The low energy part
\mathcal{K}_{1}

This section aims to establish the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of the low energy part
\mathcal{K}_{1}
. Namely,

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>0
. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
. If

\displaystyle\beta>\left\{\begin{aligned} &15,\ 0\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&19,\ 0\ is\ a\ first\ kind\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&27,\ 0\ is\ a\ second\ kind\ resonance\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

then
\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1<p<\infty
.

Before proceeding the proof, we present a crucial lemma, which plays a key role in eliminating the singularity of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
near
\mu=0
, as detailed below.

Lemma 3.2
.

(
[
28
, Lemma 4.2]
)

Let
Q,S_{j}~(j=0,1,2)
be the operators defined in Definition
2.4
. Then for any
f\in\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, the following statements hold.

(1)

(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQf)(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))\big({\bm{b_{1}(\mu)}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|}+{\bm{b_{2}(\mu)}}e^{b(\mu)|n-\rho m|}\big)d\rho

\times v_{1}(m)(Qf)(m)
,

:=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)(Qf)(m)
,

(2)

(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{j}f)(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho)\big({\bm{c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|}+{\bm{c_{2}(\mu)}}e^{b(\mu)|n-\rho m|}\big)d\rho\cdot v_{2}(m)

+{\bm{c_{3}(\mu)}}|n-m|v(m)\Big](S_{j}f)(m),

:=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)(S_{j}f)(m),

(3)

(R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{2}f)(n)=\frac{1}{8\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big[\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho)^{2}({\rm sign}(n-\rho m))^{3}\big({\bm{d_{1}(\mu)}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n-\rho m|}+{\bm{d_{2}(\mu)}}e^{b(\mu)|n-\rho m|}\big)d\rho

\times v_{3}(m)+{\bm{d_{3}(\mu)}}|n-m|v(m)\Big](S_{2}f)(m)
,

:=\frac{1}{8\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)(S_{2}f)(m)
,

(4)

Q(vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})f\big)=Qf^{\pm},\quad S_{j}\big(vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})f\big)=S_{j}f^{\pm}_{j},
where
j=0,1,2
and

•

a_{1}(\mu)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}
,
a_{2}(\mu)=\frac{-1}{\sqrt{1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4}}}
,
b(\mu)={\rm ln}\big(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}-\mu(1+\frac{\mu^{2}}{4})^{\frac{1}{2}}\big)
,

•

b_{1}(\mu)=-\theta_{+}a_{1}(\mu),\quad b_{2}(\mu)=-b(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)
,

•

c^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)=\mp i\theta^{2}_{+}a_{1}(\mu),\quad c_{2}(\mu)=(b(\mu))^{2}a_{2}(\mu),\quad c_{3}(\mu)=\theta_{+}a_{1}(\mu)+b(\mu)a_{2}(\mu)
,

•

d_{1}(\mu)=\theta^{3}_{+}a_{1}(\mu),\quad d_{2}(\mu)=-(b(\mu))^{3}a_{2}(\mu),\quad d_{3}(\mu)=2c_{3}(\mu),

•

f^{\pm}(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{B}^{\pm}(\mu,m,n)f(m),\quad f^{\pm}_{j}(n)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{C}^{\pm}(\mu,m,n)f(m),\ j=0,1,

•

f^{\pm}_{2}(n)=\frac{1}{8\mu^{3}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathcal{D}^{\pm}(\mu,m,n)f(m)
.

Remark 3.3
.

(1) Noting that
\theta_{+}
,
b(\mu)
and
c_{3}(\mu)
exhibit the following behaviors, respectively:

\theta_{+}=-\mu+o(\mu),\quad b(\mu)=-\mu+o(\mu),\quad c_{3}(\mu)=-\frac{1}{3}\mu^{3}-\frac{1}{8}\mu^{4}+O(\mu^{5}),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}.

This indicates that, compared to the free resolvent
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(\mu^{-3})
(here
O(\mu^{-3})
refers to the order of the kernel
R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},n,m)
with respect to
\mu
and the same convention applies to the following operators unless otherwise specified), the operators considered in this lemma can decrease the singularity near
\mu=0
. Precisely, we have

\displaystyle\begin{split}R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQ&=O(\mu^{-2}),\quad R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{j}=O(\mu^{-1})~(j=0,1),\quad R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{2}=O(1);\\
QvR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})&=O(\mu^{-2}),\quad S_{j}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(\mu^{-1})~(j=0,1),\quad S_{2}vR^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4})=O(1).\end{split}

(3.1)

(2) However, the form in our discrete setting is far more intricate than its continuous counterpart
[
44
, Lemma 2.5]
. Specifically, compared (
2.4
) with the kernel on the line:

R^{\pm}_{0}(\mu^{4},x,y)=\frac{1}{4\mu^{3}}\big(\pm ie^{\pm i\mu|x-y|}-e^{-\mu|x-y|}\big),\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R},

we observe that the continuous analogue of
(\theta_{+},b(\mu),a_{1}(\mu),a_{2}(\mu))
is
(-\mu,-\mu,1,-1)
. This means that the corresponding
{b}_{j}(\mu)
,
{c}^{\pm}_{1}(\mu)
,
{c}_{j}(\mu)
,
{d}_{j}(\mu)
in the continuous case are the polynomials of
\mu
. In particular,
c_{3}(\mu)
vanishes identically. We remark that such discrepancy will introduce some additional technical challenges in establishing the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of
W_{+}
in our discrete setting.

3.1.
0
is a regular point of
H

In this subsection, we prove the
\ell^{p}
boundedness for
\mathcal{K}_{1}
when
0
is a regular point of
H
. First recall from (
1.11
) that

\mathcal{K}_{1}=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{1}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vM^{-1}(\mu)v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big]d\mu,

(3.2)

and the expansion (
(i)
) of
M^{-1}(\mu)
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}M^{-1}(\mu)=S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}+\mu QA_{1}Q+\mu^{2}(QA^{0}_{21}Q+S_{0}A^{0}_{22}+A^{0}_{23}S_{0})+\mu^{3}(QA^{0}_{31}+A^{0}_{32}Q)+\mu^{3}P_{1}+\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu),\end{split}

then
\mathcal{K}_{1}
can be written as a finite sum of the following integral operators:

\mathcal{K}_{1}=\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}}K_{A}+K_{1}+K_{P_{1}}+K^{0}_{4},

(3.3)

where
\mathcal{A}_{0}=\{S_{0}A_{0}S_{0},\mu^{2}QA^{0}_{21}Q,\mu^{2}S_{0}A^{0}_{22},\mu^{2}A^{0}_{23}S_{0},\mu^{3}QA^{0}_{31},\mu^{3}A^{0}_{32}Q\}
and

\displaystyle K_{A}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{1}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vAv(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,\quad A\in\mathcal{A}_{0},

\displaystyle K_{1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{4}\chi_{1}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vQA_{1}Qv(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

(3.4)

\displaystyle K_{P_{1}}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{6}\chi_{1}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vP_{1}v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu,

(3.5)

\displaystyle K^{0}_{4}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{1}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\Gamma^{0}_{4}(\mu)v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

(3.6)

Based on (
3.1
), we can classify the operators in (
3.3
) into the following two groups according to the order of their kernels with respect to
\mu
as
\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}
:

\displaystyle O(1):K~(K\in\{K_{1},K_{P_{1}}\}),\quad O(\mu):K~(K\in\{K^{0}_{4}\}\cup\{K_{A}:A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}\}).

The
\ell^{p}
boundedness of
\mathcal{K}_{1}
consequently reduces to proving the boundedness of these two operator classes. We will establish this through three propositions.

To begin with, we deal with the operators in the class
O(\mu)
. Prior to this, we give the following Schur test lemma, which will often be used to establish the
\ell^{p}
-boundedness of integral operators.

Lemma 3.4
.

If the kernel
K(n,m)
satisfies

\sup_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}|K(n,m)|+\sup_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}|K(n,m)|<\infty,

then
{K}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty.

In particular, as a sufficient condition of this lemma
|K(n,m)|\lesssim\left<|n|-|m|\right>^{-\gamma}
with
\gamma>1
will be used often in the proof.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>15
. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
and
0
is a regular point of
H
. Let
\mathcal{A}_{0}
be defined in (
3.3
), then for any
A\in\mathcal{A}_{0}
,
K_{A}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

Proof.

(1)
For
A=S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}
, denote

\displaystyle K_{0}(n,m)=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{1}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vS_{0}A_{0}S_{0}v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

By virtue of Lemma
3.2
, it can be further expressed as

\displaystyle K_{0}(n,m)=\frac{1}{16}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}(K^{+,j}_{0}+K^{-,j}_{0})(n,m),

(3.7)

where
N_{1}=n-\rho_{1}m_{1}
,
M_{2}=m-\rho_{2}m_{2}
and

\displaystyle K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{-3}(c^{+}_{1}(\mu))^{2}\chi_{1}(\mu)\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(1-\rho_{1})(1-\rho_{2})e^{-i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|\pm|M_{2}|)}d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times(v_{2}S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2})d\mu,

\displaystyle K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{-3}c^{+}_{1}(\mu)c_{2}(\mu)\chi_{1}(\mu)\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(1-\rho_{1})(1-\rho_{2})e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|\pm i\theta_{+}|M_{2}|}d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times(v_{2}S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2})d\mu,

\displaystyle K^{\pm,3}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{-3}c^{+}_{1}(\mu)c_{3}(\mu)\chi_{1}(\mu)\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho_{2})e^{\pm i\theta_{+}|M_{2}|}d\rho_{2}\cdot|n-m_{1}|

\displaystyle\quad\times(vS_{0}A_{0}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2})d\mu.

Next we establish the following estimates:

|K^{\pm,j}_{0}(n,m)|\lesssim\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2},\quad j=1,2,3,

(3.8)

which combined with Lemma
3.4
and (
3.7
), yield that
K_{0}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for any
1\leq p\leq\infty
.

\bm{Case\ j=1.}
Decomposing

e^{-i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|\pm|M_{2}|)}=e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}e^{-i\theta_{+}\big(|N_{1}|-|n|\pm(|M_{2}|-|m|)\big)}

(3.9)

and employing the following variable substitution:

{\rm cos}\theta_{+}=1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{2}\Longrightarrow\ \frac{d\mu}{d\theta_{+}}=\frac{{\rm sin}\theta_{+}}{\mu},\quad\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 0\ {\rm and}\ \theta_{+}\rightarrow-\pi\ {\rm as}\ \mu\rightarrow 2,

(3.10)

we can rewrite
K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)
as

\displaystyle K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}\mu^{-3}\theta^{4}_{+}\chi_{11}(\mu)L^{\pm,1}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}g(\theta_{+})\chi_{11}(\mu(\theta_{+}))L^{\pm,1}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+}

(3.11)

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}G^{\pm,1}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+},

where
\chi_{11}(\mu)=-\chi_{1}(\mu)(1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4})^{-1}
,
g(\theta_{+})=-\Big(\frac{\theta^{2}_{+}}{2(1-{\rm cos}\theta_{+})}\Big)^{2}{\rm sin}\theta_{+}
and

\displaystyle L^{\pm,1}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(1-\rho_{1})(1-\rho_{2})e^{-i\theta_{+}\big(|N_{1}|-|n|\pm(|M_{2}|-|m|)\big)}d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times(v_{2}S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}).

First, for each
k=0,1,2
, we have the following estimate:

\sup\limits_{\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)}\big|(\partial^{k}_{\theta_{+}}L^{\pm,1}_{0})(\theta_{+},n,m)\big|\lesssim\|\left<\cdot\right>^{2k+4}V(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}},\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.12)

This estimate combined with the facts that
supp\chi_{1}(\mu)\subseteq[0,\mu_{0}]
,
\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}g(\theta_{+})=0
immediately yields

|K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)|\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.13)

Moreover, applying integration by parts twice to
K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)
with
||n|\pm|m||\geq 1
, we obtain

\displaystyle K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)=\Big(\frac{e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}}{-i(|n|\pm|m|)}G^{\pm,1}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)\Big)\Big|^{0}_{\theta_{+}=-\pi}-\int_{-\pi}^{0}\frac{e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}}{-i(|n|\pm|m|)}(\partial_{\theta_{+}}G^{\pm,1}_{0})(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{i(|n|\pm|m|)}\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}(\partial_{\theta_{+}}G^{\pm,1}_{0})(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(|n|\pm|m|)^{2}}\Big(\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}(\partial_{\theta_{+}}G^{\pm,1}_{0})(\theta_{+},n,m)-\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|n|\pm|m|)}(\partial^{2}_{\theta_{+}}G^{\pm,1}_{0})(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+}\Big)

\displaystyle=O((|n|\pm|m|)^{-2}),

where the second equality follows from the support condition of
\chi_{1}(\mu)
and
\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}g(\theta_{+})=0
. The fourth equality is obtained by combining the support of
\chi_{1}(\mu)
, the estimate (
3.12
) and the existence of limits
\lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}g^{(k)}(\theta_{+})
for
k=1,2
. Therefore, for any
n,m\in\mathbb{Z}
, one has

\displaystyle|K^{\pm,1}_{0}(n,m)|\lesssim\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2}.

\bm{Case\ j=2.}
We consider the decomposition

e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|\pm i\theta_{+}|M_{2}|}=e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}e^{b(\mu)(|N_{1}|-|n|)\pm i\theta_{+}(|M_{2}|-|m|)},

(3.14)

then
K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}\mu^{-3}(b(\mu))^{2}\theta^{2}_{+}\chi_{12}(\mu)L^{\pm,2}_{0}(\mu,n,m)d\mu

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}G^{\pm,2}_{0}(\mu,n,m)d\mu,

(3.15)

where
\chi_{12}(\mu)=i\chi_{1}(\mu)(1-\frac{\mu^{4}}{16})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and

\displaystyle L^{\pm,2}_{0}(\mu,n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(1-\rho_{1})(1-\rho_{2})e^{b(\mu)(|N_{1}|-|n|)\pm i\theta_{+}(|M_{2}|-|m|)}d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\quad\times(v_{2}S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}).

Noting that
b(\mu)<0
,
\theta^{\prime}_{+}(\mu)=-(1-\frac{\mu^{2}}{4})^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and

b^{\prime}(\mu)=-(2+\mu^{2})^{-1}\big((4+\mu^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mu^{2}(4+\mu^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big)<0,\quad\mu\in(0,2),

we can verify that for any
k=0,1,2
,

\displaystyle\sup\limits_{\mu\in(0,\mu_{0}]}\big|e^{b(\mu)|n|}(\partial^{k}_{\mu}L^{\pm,2}_{0})(\mu,n,m)\big|\lesssim\|\left<\cdot\right>^{2k+4}V(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}},\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.16)

This immediately yields that
K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)
is uniformly bounded on
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
by combining
supp\chi_{1}(\mu)\subseteq[0,\mu_{0}]
and the existence of the limits

\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{b(\mu)}{\mu}
and
\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{\theta_{+}}{\mu}.
On the other hand, assuming that
||n|\pm|m||\geq 1
, we apply integration by parts twice to
K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)
obtaining that

\displaystyle K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)=\Big(e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}\frac{G^{\pm,2}_{0}(\mu,n,m)}{\alpha^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)}\Big)\Big|^{2}_{\mu=0}-\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}\Big(\frac{G^{\pm,2}_{0}}{\alpha^{\pm}}\Big)^{\prime}(\mu,n,m)d\mu

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\to}^{\widetilde{G}^{\pm,2}_{0}:=\Big(\frac{G^{\pm,2}_{0}}{\alpha^{\pm}}\Big)^{\prime}}}-\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}\widetilde{G}^{\pm,2}_{0}(\mu,n,m)d\mu

\displaystyle=\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{G}^{\pm,2}_{0}}{\alpha^{\pm}}\Big)(\mu,n,m)+\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta_{+}|m|}\Big(\frac{\widetilde{G}^{\pm,2}_{0}}{\alpha^{\pm}}\Big)^{\prime}(\mu,n,m)d\mu

\displaystyle=O((|n|\pm|m|)^{-2}),

where

\alpha^{\pm}(\mu,n,m):=b^{\prime}(\mu)|n|\pm i\theta^{\prime}_{+}(\mu)|m|,

(3.17)

and in the second equality we used the facts that
supp\chi_{1}(\mu)\subseteq[0,\mu_{0}]
,
\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{(b(\mu))^{2}\theta^{2}_{+}}{\mu^{3}}=0
and (
3.16
). To verify the fourth equality, first, we compute

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\widetilde{G}^{\pm,2}_{0}}{\alpha^{\pm}}\Big)(\mu,n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\alpha^{\pm})^{2}}\Big(\frac{-(\alpha^{\pm})^{\prime}}{\alpha^{\pm}}G^{\pm,2}_{0}+(G^{\pm,2}_{0})^{\prime}\Big)(\mu,n,m),

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{\widetilde{G}^{\pm,2}_{0}}{\alpha^{\pm}}\Big)^{\prime}(\mu,n,m)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\alpha^{\pm})^{2}}\Big[\Big(-\frac{(\alpha^{\pm})^{(2)}}{\alpha^{\pm}}+3\Big(\frac{(\alpha^{\pm})^{\prime}}{\alpha^{\pm}}\Big)^{2}\Big)G^{\pm,2}_{0}-3\frac{(\alpha^{\pm})^{\prime}}{\alpha^{\pm}}(G^{\pm,2}_{0})^{\prime}+(G^{\pm,2}_{0})^{(2)}\Big](\mu,n,m).

Notice that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\alpha^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)|^{2}}\lesssim(|n|+|m|)^{-2},\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ (n,m)\neq(0,0)\ {\rm and}\ \mu\in(0,2),

and for any
k=1,2
,

\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\Big(\frac{b(\mu)}{\mu}\Big)^{(k)}\ {\rm and}\ \lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\Big(\frac{\theta_{+}}{\mu}\Big)^{(k)}\ {\rm exist},

\Big|\frac{(\partial^{k}_{\mu}\alpha^{\pm})(\mu,n,m)}{\alpha^{\pm}(\mu,n,m)}\Big|\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ (n,m)\neq(0,0)\ {\rm and}\ \mu\in(0,\mu_{0}].

These facts together with (
3.16
) establish the fourth equality, which combined with the uniform boundedness of
K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)
gives

\displaystyle|K^{\pm,2}_{0}(n,m)|\lesssim\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2},\quad\forall\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

\bm{Case\ j=3.}
Considering

e^{\pm i\theta_{+}|M_{2}|}=e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|n|+|m|)}e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|M_{2}|-(|n|+|m|))},

which allows us to rewrite
K^{\pm,3}_{0}(n,m)
as

\displaystyle K^{\pm,3}_{0}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|n|+|m|)}\mu^{-3}c^{+}_{1}(\mu)c_{3}(\mu){\chi}_{1}(\mu)L^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\mu

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\to}^{{\rm by}\ \eqref{varible substi1}}}\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|n|+|m|)}{\chi}_{13}(\mu(\theta_{+}))\frac{\theta^{2}_{+}}{2(1-{\rm cos}\theta_{+})}L^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+}

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\pi}^{0}e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|n|+|m|)}G^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)d\theta_{+},

(3.18)

where
{\chi}_{13}(\mu)=-i\chi_{1}(\mu)\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu}
and

\displaystyle L^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho_{2})e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|M_{2}|-|m|-|n|)}d\rho_{2}\cdot|n-m_{1}|\big(vS_{0}A_{0}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}).

In view that

c_{3}(\mu)=-\frac{1}{3}\mu^{3}-\frac{1}{8}\mu^{4}+O(\mu^{5}),\quad\mu\rightarrow 0^{+},

and for any
k=0,1,2
,

\lim\limits_{\mu\rightarrow 0^{+}}\Big(\frac{c_{3}(\mu)}{\mu}\Big)^{(k)}\quad{\rm and}\ \lim\limits_{\theta_{+}\rightarrow 0}\Big(\frac{\theta^{2}_{+}}{2(1-{\rm cos}\theta_{+})}\Big)^{(k)}\ {\rm exist}.

According to the argument for
K^{\pm,1}_{0}
, it suffices to establish the following estimate for any
k=0,1,2
:

\sup\limits_{\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)}\big|(\partial^{k}_{\theta_{+}}L^{\pm,3}_{0})(\theta_{+},n,m)\big|\lesssim 1,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.19)

To see this,
for
{\bm{k=0}}
, using the orthogonality
\left<S_{0}f,v\right>=0
, we have

L^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n|}(|n-m_{1}|-|n|)v(m_{1})(S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}(h^{\pm}(\theta_{+},m,\cdot)))(m_{1})

with

h^{\pm}(\theta_{+},m,m_{2})=v_{2}(m_{2})\int_{0}^{1}(1-\rho_{2})e^{\pm i\theta_{+}(|M_{2}|-|m|)}d\rho_{2}.

By the triangle inequality and Hölder’s inequality, we obtain

\sup\limits_{\theta_{+}\in(-\pi,0)}|L^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)|\lesssim\|\left<\cdot\right>^{4}V(\cdot)\|_{\ell^{1}},\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ n,m\in\mathbb{Z}.

(3.20)

For
{\bm{k=1}}
, it is crucial to show that

\tilde{L}^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m):=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n|}|n|\cdot|n-m_{1}|v(m_{1})(S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}(h^{\pm}(\theta_{+},m,\cdot)))(m_{1})

is uniformly bounded in
n,m,\theta_{+}
. Using
\left<S_{0}f,v\right>=\left<S_{0}f,v_{1}\right>=0
, we rewrite it as

\displaystyle\tilde{L}^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n|}\underbrace{(|n|\cdot|n-m_{1}|-n^{2}+nm_{1})}_{J_{1}(n,m_{1})}v(m_{1})(S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}(h^{\pm}(\theta_{+},m,\cdot)))(m_{1}),

which together with the following fact:

\displaystyle|J_{1}(n,m_{1})|=\big||n|(|n-m_{1}|-|n|)+nm_{1}\big|=\Big|\frac{|n|m^{2}_{1}+nm_{1}(|n-m_{1}|-|n|)}{|n-m_{1}|+|n|}\Big|\lesssim\left<m_{1}\right>^{2}.

gives the desired uniform boundedness.
For
{\bm{k=2}}
, it is key to verify the uniform boundedness of

\displaystyle\tilde{\tilde{L}}^{\pm,3}_{0}(\theta_{+},n,m)

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n|}|n|^{2}\cdot|n-m_{1}|v(m_{1})(S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}(h^{\pm}(\theta_{+},m,\cdot)))(m_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}e^{\mp i\theta_{+}|n|}\underbrace{(|n|^{2}\cdot|n-m_{1}|-n^{2}|n|+|n|nm_{1})}_{J_{2}(n,m_{1})}v(m_{1})(S_{0}A_{0}S_{0}(h^{\pm}(\theta_{+},m,\cdot)))(m_{1}).

This can be obtained by the fact that

|J_{2}(n,m_{1})|\lesssim\left<m_{1}\right>^{3},

which can be verified through the following computation:

\displaystyle J_{2}(n,m_{1})

\displaystyle=n^{2}(|n-m_{1}|-|n|)+|n|nm_{1}=\frac{n^{2}m^{2}_{1}+n|n|m_{1}(|n-m_{1}|-|n|)}{|n-m_{1}|+|n|}
