1<p<\infty
.

(3)
For
B=B^{1}_{02}\widetilde{S}_{0}
, combining (
5.7
) and the method used for
k^{\pm,j}_{-1}
, similarly, we can derive

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{K}_{02}(n,m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{3}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})vB^{1}_{02}\widetilde{S}_{0}v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu

\displaystyle=-\frac{(-1)^{n+m}}{64}h_{1,0}(n,m)\widetilde{C}_{02}(m)+O(\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2}),

where
h_{1,0}(n,m)
is defined in (
3.50
) and

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{02}(m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(M_{2}))d\rho_{2}\cdot(\tilde{v}B^{1}_{02}\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}_{1})(m_{1},m_{2}).

Thus,
\widetilde{K}_{02}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1<p<\infty
and we complete the whole proof.
∎

5.3.
16
is an eigenvalue of
H

Finally, in this subsection, we deal with the case where
16
is an eigenvalue of
H
. From the expansion (
(vi)
)

\displaystyle M^{-1}(\mu)

\displaystyle=(2-\mu)^{-1}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}+(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\big(\widetilde{S}_{0}B_{-1,1}\widetilde{Q}+\widetilde{Q}B_{-1,2}\widetilde{S}_{0}\big)+\big(\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{01}+B^{2}_{02}\widetilde{Q}\big)

\displaystyle\quad+(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}(\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{11}+B^{2}_{12}\widetilde{Q})+(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{P}_{1}+(2-\mu)B^{2}_{21}+\Gamma^{2}_{\frac{3}{2}}(2-\mu),

then
\mathcal{K}_{3}
can be written as

\mathcal{K}_{3}=\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}\cup\mathcal{B}_{22}}K_{B}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+K^{2}_{r},

(5.20)

where
\mathcal{B}_{21}=\{(2-\mu)^{-1}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1},(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{S}_{0}B_{-1,1}\widetilde{Q},(2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{Q}B_{-1,2}\widetilde{S}_{0},\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{01},B^{2}_{02}\widetilde{Q}\}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{22}

\displaystyle=\{(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{11},(2-\mu)^{\frac{1}{2}}B^{2}_{12}\widetilde{Q},(2-\mu)B^{2}_{21}\},

\displaystyle K^{2}_{r}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}\mu^{3}\chi_{3}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\Gamma^{2}_{\frac{3}{2}}(2-\mu)v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

Recall from the established result in Propositions
5.4
and
5.6
, we have

•

K\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))\ {\rm for\ all\ }1\leq p\leq\infty
with
K\in\{K^{2}_{r}\}\cup\{K_{B}\}_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{22}}
,

•

K_{B},K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))\ {\rm for\ all\ }1<p<\infty
with
B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}\setminus\{(2-\mu)^{-1}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}\}
.

Hence, it remains to prove that
\widetilde{K}_{-2}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1<p<\infty
, where

\displaystyle\bm{\widetilde{K}_{-2}(n,m)}

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{2}(2-\mu)^{-1}\mu^{3}\chi_{3}(\mu)\big[R^{+}_{0}(\mu^{4})v\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}v(R^{+}_{0}-R^{-}_{0})(\mu^{4})\big](n,m)d\mu.

To see this, we first note that from Lemma
5.2
,
\widetilde{K}_{-2}=O((2-\mu)^{-\frac{1}{2}})
as
\mu\rightarrow 2
. Through a similar argument as
\widetilde{K}_{01}
in Proposition
5.6
, one can obtain

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{-2}(n,m)=\frac{(-1)^{n+m}}{32}\widetilde{C}_{-2}(n)g_{1,0}(n,m)+R_{-2}(n,m),

(5.21)

where the integral operator
{R}_{-2}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{-2}(n)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{0}^{1}({\rm sign}(N_{1}))d\rho_{1}(\tilde{v}_{1}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}).

(5.22)

This gives the desired result. Therefore, to sum up, we have the following conclusion.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>17
. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
and
16
is an eigenvalue of
H
. Then
\mathcal{K}_{3}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1<p<\infty
.

Hence, this together with Propositions
5.4
and
5.6
completes the whole proof of Theorem
5.1
.

6.
Counterexample for the boundedness at endpoints

In this section, we establish the unboundedness of the wave operators
W_{\pm}
at endpoints
p=1,\infty
, i.e., Theorem
1.4
. As before, we focus our analysis on
W_{+}
. Prior to the proof, we state our strategy. Recall from (
1.10
) that
W_{+}
is given by

W_{+}=I-\frac{2}{\pi i}\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}\mathcal{K}_{j}.

By Theorem
4.1
,
\mathcal{K}_{2}
is always bounded on
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
. Consequently, the unboundedness of
W_{+}
at
p=1
and
p=\infty
reduces to analyzing the behaviors of the remaining low energy part
\mathcal{K}_{1}
and high energy part
\mathcal{K}_{3}
. Building on the results from Sections
3
and
5
, we can further reduce this analysis to studying the key operator
\mathcal{K}
, whose specific form depends on resonance types, as detailed below.

Case (I): Assume that
0
is a regular point of
H
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=K_{P_{1}}+K_{1}+\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}},\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{-1}+\widetilde{K}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{02},\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}}K_{B},\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(6.1)

where
K_{1},K_{P_{1}},K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}
are defined in (
3.3
), (
5.4
), respectively,
\widetilde{K}_{-1}
,
\widetilde{K}_{01}
,
\widetilde{K}_{02}
are defined in Proposition
5.6
and
\mathcal{B}_{21}
is defined in (
5.20
).

Case (II): Assume that
0
is a first kind resonance of
H
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=K_{P_{1}}+\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{11}}K_{A}+\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}},\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{-1}+\widetilde{K}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{02},\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}}K_{B},\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(6.2)

where sets
\mathcal{A}_{11}
and
\mathcal{B}_{21}
are defined in (
3.46
) and (
5.20
), respectively.

Case (III): Assume that
0
is a second kind resonance of
H
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=K_{P_{1}}+\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{21}}K_{A}+\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}},\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{-1}+\widetilde{K}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{02},\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}}K_{B},\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(6.3)

where
\mathcal{A}_{21}
is defined in (
3.52
).

Throughout this section, we always choose the characteristic functions
f_{N}(n):=\chi_{[-N,N]}(n)
on the interval
[-N,N]
with
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
as test functions. The proof of Theorem
1.4
will be divided into the following four propositions.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
with
|V(n)|\lesssim\left<n\right>^{-\beta}
for
\beta>15
. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
and
both
{\bm{0}}
and
{\bm{16}}
are regular points of
{\bm{H}}
, then

(1)

|(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}})f_{N}(N+2)|\rightarrow+\infty,N\rightarrow+\infty
and
(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}})f_{1}\notin\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
,

(2)

\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|K_{1}f_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty
and
K_{1}f_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

In particular,
\mathcal{K}=K_{P_{1}}+K_{1}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}
is neither bounded on
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
nor on
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

(1)
It follows from (
3.38
) and (
5.14
) that

\displaystyle(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}})(n,m)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{i-1}{8}+\frac{(-1)^{n+m}}{4}\Big)(k^{+}_{1}+k^{-}_{1})(n,m)+\frac{i-1}{8}(k^{+}_{2}+k^{-}_{2})(n,m)

\displaystyle\quad+O(\left<|n|\pm|m|\right>^{-2}).

By virtue of the uniform boundedness of
k^{\pm}_{\ell}(n,m)
, we can further decomposition it as

\displaystyle k^{\pm}_{\ell}(n,m)=(\mathbf{1}_{E}+\mathbf{1}_{E^{c}})(n,m)k^{\pm}_{\ell}(n,m)=\mathbf{1}_{E}(n,m)k^{\pm}_{\ell}(n,m)+O(\left<|n|-|m|\right>^{-2}),\quad\ell=1,2,

(6.4)

where
\mathbf{1}_{E}
denotes the characteristic function on the set
E=\{(x,y):||x|-|y||\geq 2\}
and
E^{c}
corresponds to the complementary set.
This together with the definition of
\phi
in Lemma
3.7
allows us to rewrite the kernel of
K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}
as

\displaystyle(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}})(n,m)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{i-1}{8}+\frac{(-1)^{n+m}}{4}\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{|n|+|m|}+\frac{1}{|n|-|m|}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{E}(n,m)+\frac{(i-1)|n|}{4(n^{2}+m^{2})}\mathbf{1}_{E}(n,m)

\displaystyle\quad+O(\left<|n|-|m|\right>^{-2})

\displaystyle:=G_{1}(n,m)+G_{2}(n,m)+R(n,m).

(6.5)

Notice that the integral operator
G_{2}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z}))
through the following estimate

\sup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}|G_{2}(n,m)|\lesssim\sup\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{|n|}{n^{2}+m^{2}}<\infty.

(6.6)

This observation and
R\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
, indicates that it suffices to establish

|(G_{1}f_{N})(N+2)|\rightarrow+\infty,N\rightarrow+\infty\ {\rm and}\ (G_{1}+G_{2})f_{1}\notin\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}).

Indeed, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, a direct calculation yields that

\displaystyle(G_{1}f_{N})(N+2)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m=-N}^{N}\Big(\frac{i-1}{8}+\frac{(-1)^{N+2+m}}{4}\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{N+2+|m|}+\frac{1}{N+2-|m|}\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{i-1}{4}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{2N+2}\frac{1}{k}+\frac{1}{2}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{2N+2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k},

and thus
|(G_{1}f_{N})(N+2)|=+\infty
as
N\rightarrow+\infty
. For the latter, we have

\displaystyle\|(G_{1}+G_{2})f_{1}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\big|\sum\limits_{m=-1}^{1}(G_{1}+G_{2})(n,m)\big|\gtrsim\sum\limits_{n=3}^{+\infty}\sum\limits_{m=-1}^{1}\Big(\frac{1}{n+|m|}+\frac{1}{n-|m|}+\frac{2n}{n^{2}+m^{2}}\Big)

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{n=3}^{+\infty}\sum\limits_{m=-1}^{1}\Big(\frac{1}{n+|m|}+\frac{1}{n-|m|}\Big)\gtrsim\sum\limits_{k=4}^{+\infty}\frac{1}{k}=+\infty.

(2)
Recall from the (
3.39
), through rewriting
K^{\pm,j}_{1}(n,m)
as

\displaystyle K^{\pm,j}_{1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})\int_{[0,1]^{2}}({\rm sign}(N_{1}))({\rm sign}(M_{2}))k^{\pm,j}_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2},

with

\displaystyle k^{\pm,1}_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{-i\theta_{+}(|N_{1}|\pm|M_{2}|)}\chi_{1}(\mu)\frac{\theta^{2}_{+}}{({\rm sin}\theta_{+})^{2}}d\mu,

\displaystyle k^{\pm,2}_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{2}e^{b(\mu)|N_{1}|\pm i\theta_{+}|M_{2}|}\chi_{1}(\mu)\frac{a_{2}(\mu)b(\mu)\theta_{+}}{\mu{\rm sin}\theta_{+}}d\mu,

and then applying the argument used for
K^{\pm,j}_{P_{1}}
to
k^{\pm,j}_{1}
and the estimate (
5.17
), we have

\displaystyle 16K_{1}(n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})\int_{[0,1]^{2}}k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}+R_{1}(n,m),

where
R_{1}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
and

k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})=({\rm sign}(N_{1}))({\rm sign}(M_{2}))h_{i,1}(N_{1},M_{2}).

Utilizing the decomposition (
6.4
) and the estimate (
5.17
) again, it further reduces to show that

\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|\widetilde{K}_{1}f_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty\ {\rm and\ }\widetilde{K}_{1}f_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}),

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}_{1}(n,m)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\mathbf{1}_{E}(N_{1},M_{2})k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}.

To see this, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, we decompose
\mathbb{Z}^{2}=D_{N}\cup D^{c}_{N}
with

D_{N}=\{(m_{1},m_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}^{2}~\big||m_{1}|\geq\frac{N}{2}\ {\rm or}\ |m_{2}|\geq\frac{N}{2}\},

then for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle|(\widetilde{K}_{1}f_{N})(n)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum\limits_{m=-N}^{N}\sum\limits_{(m_{1},m_{2})\in D_{N}}|(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})|\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\big|k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})\big|d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum\limits_{(m_{1},m_{2})\in D^{c}_{N}}|(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})|\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\Big|\sum\limits_{m=-N}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{E}(N_{1},M_{2})k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})\Big|d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle:=K^{(1)}_{N}(n)+K^{(2)}_{N}(n).

(i)
For
K^{(1)}_{N}(n)
, by virtue of the uniform boundedness of
k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})
, it yields that

\displaystyle K^{(1)}_{N}(n)\lesssim\sum\limits_{m=-N}^{N}\Big(\sum\limits_{|m_{1}|\geq\frac{N}{2}}\sum\limits_{m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}+\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{|m_{2}|\geq\frac{N}{2}}\Big)|(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})|

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim{N}\Big[\sum\limits_{|m_{1}|\geq\frac{N}{2}}\sum\limits_{m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\big|(\frac{1}{N}\left<\cdot\right>v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})\big|+\sum\limits_{m_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{|m_{2}|\geq\frac{N}{2}}\big|(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1}\left<\cdot\right>\frac{1}{N})(m_{1},m_{2})\big|\Big]

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}|\big(\left<\cdot\right>v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1}\left<\cdot\right>\big)(m_{1},m_{2})|<\infty.

(ii)
For
K^{(2)}_{N}(n)
, noting that
k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})
is an odd function about
M_{2}
, then for any
|m_{2}|\leq\frac{N}{2}
, the sum
\sum\limits_{m=-N}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{E}(N_{1},M_{2})k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})
contains at most
2|m_{2}|
terms. This combined with the uniform boundedness of
k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})
yields that

\displaystyle\Big|\sum\limits_{m=-N}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{E}(N_{1},M_{2})k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})\Big|\lesssim\left<m_{2}\right>,\quad{\rm uniformly\ in}\ m,m_{1},\rho_{1},\rho_{2},N.

Hence,
K^{(2)}_{N}(n)\lesssim 1
uniformly in
n
and
N
and this establishes
\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|\widetilde{K}_{1}f_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty
.

On the other hand, basing on

\displaystyle|\mathbf{1}_{E}(N_{1},M_{2})k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})|\lesssim|M_{2}|\left<|N_{1}|-|M_{2}|\right>^{-2},

and the estimate (
5.17
), we have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{K}_{1}f_{1}\|_{\ell^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}|(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})|\int_{[0,1]^{2}}\sum\limits_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum\limits_{m=-1}^{1}|\mathbf{1}_{E}(N_{1},M_{2})k_{1}(N_{1},M_{2})|d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\left<m_{2}\right>|(v_{1}QA_{1}Qv_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})|<\infty,

and this completes the whole proof.

∎

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
and
V
be compactly supported. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
and
{\bm{0}}

is a regular point of

{\bm{H}}
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)
if
{\bm{16}}

is a resonance of

{\bm{H}}
, then

•

for any
K\in\{K_{1},\widetilde{K}_{-1},\widetilde{K}_{02}\}
,
\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|Kf_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty
and
Kf_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
,

•

\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}|(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{01})f_{N}(N+2)|=\infty
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{C}_{1}\neq 16(1\mp 3\sqrt{2})
, then
(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{01})f_{1}\notin\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
,

where

\mathcal{C}_{1}=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(\tilde{v}_{1}\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{1}_{01}\tilde{v})(m_{1},m_{2}).

(6.7)

In particular,
\mathcal{K}=K_{P_{1}}+K_{1}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{-1}+\widetilde{K}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{02}
is unbounded on
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
, and if additionally
\mathcal{C}_{1}\neq 16(1\mp 3\sqrt{2})
, then
\mathcal{K}
is unbounded on
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

(2)
If
{\bm{16}}

is an eigenvalue of

{\bm{H}}
, then

•

for any
K\in\{K_{1}\}\cup\{K_{B}:B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}\setminus\{\widetilde{K}_{-2},\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01}\}\}
,
\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|Kf_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty
and
Kf_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
,

•

\lim\limits_{N\rightarrow\infty}|(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{-2})f_{N}(N+2)|=\infty
. Moreover, if
\mathcal{C}_{2}\neq 16(1\mp 3\sqrt{2})
, then
(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{-2})f_{1}\not\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
,

where
\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01}=K_{B}
with
B=\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{01}
and

\mathcal{C}_{2}=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(\tilde{v}_{1}\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{01}\tilde{v})(m_{1},m_{2})-2\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(\tilde{v}_{1}\widetilde{S}_{1}B_{-2}\widetilde{S}_{1}\tilde{v}_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}).

(6.8)

Therefore,
\mathcal{K}=K_{P_{1}}+K_{1}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}}K_{B}
is unbounded on
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
, and if additionally
\mathcal{C}_{2}\neq 16(1\mp 3\sqrt{2})
, then
\mathcal{K}
is unbounded on
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

(1)

Step 1:
For the first item, combining Proposition
6.1
, it remains to prove

\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|Kf_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty\ and\ Kf_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}),\quad K=\widetilde{K}_{-1},\widetilde{K}_{02}.

(6.9)

Basing on Proposition
5.6
, we reformulate
\widetilde{K}^{\pm,j}_{-1}(n,m)
and
\widetilde{K}_{02}(n,m)
as the form of
\widetilde{K}^{\pm,2}_{01}
, then an analogous argument yields that

\displaystyle 16\widetilde{K}_{-1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{K}^{(1)}_{-1}+\widetilde{K}^{(2)}_{-1})(n,m)+R_{-1}(n,m),

\displaystyle-64\widetilde{K}_{02}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(\tilde{v}B^{1}_{02}\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}_{2})(m_{1},m_{2})\int_{0}^{1}(-1)^{n+\rho_{2}m_{2}}k_{02}(\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})d\rho_{2}+R_{02}(n,m),

where
R_{-1},R_{02}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for
1\leq p\leq\infty
and

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{(1)}_{-1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(\tilde{v}_{1}\widetilde{S}_{0}B_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})\int_{[0,1]^{2}}(-1)^{n+\rho_{2}m_{2}}{k}^{(1)}_{-1}(N_{1},M_{2})d\rho_{1}d\rho_{2},

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}^{(2)}_{-1}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v\widetilde{S}_{0}B_{-1}\widetilde{S}_{0}\tilde{v}_{1})(m_{1},m_{2})\int_{0}^{1}(-1)^{\rho_{2}m_{2}}{k}^{(2)}_{-1}(\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})d\rho_{2},

with

\displaystyle k_{02}(\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{M_{2}}({\rm sign}(M_{2}))h_{1,0}(\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2}),

\displaystyle{k}^{(1)}_{-1}(N_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{-i}{2}(-1)^{M_{2}}({\rm sign}(N_{1}))({\rm sign}(M_{2}))h_{1,0}({N}_{1},M_{2}),

\displaystyle{k}^{(2)}_{-1}(\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{8}q^{|\widetilde{N}_{1}|}(-1)^{M_{2}}({\rm sign}(M_{2}))h_{0,1}(\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2}).

Since all
k_{02},k^{(1)}_{-1},k^{(2)}_{-1}
are uniformly bounded in
N_{1},\widetilde{N}_{1},M_{2}
and are odd functions about
M_{2}
, then the argument for
{K}_{1}
in Proposition
6.1
is valid for
\widetilde{K}_{-1}
and
\widetilde{K}_{02}
, and thus the desired result (
6.9
) is obtained.

Step 2:
For the second item, from (
6
) and (
5.19
), we have

\displaystyle(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{01})(n,m)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{i-1}{8}+(-1)^{n+m}\Big(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\widetilde{C}^{1}_{01}(n)}{64}\Big)\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{|n|+|m|}+\frac{1}{|n|-|m|}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{E}(n,m)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(i-1)|n|}{4(n^{2}+m^{2})}\mathbf{1}_{E}(n,m)+\Big(O(\left<|n|-|m|\right>^{-2})+\widetilde{R}_{01}(n,m)\Big)

\displaystyle:=G^{(1)}_{1}(n,m)+G^{(1)}_{2}(n,m)+R^{(1)}(n,m).

It suffices to show that

|(G^{(1)}_{1}f_{N})(N+2)|\rightarrow\infty,N\rightarrow\infty,\ and\ (G^{(1)}_{1}+G^{(1)}_{2})f_{1}\notin\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})\ if\ \mathcal{C}_{1}\neq 16(1\mp 3\sqrt{2}).

(6.10)

Since
V
is compactly supported, that is, there exists an integer
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, such that
suppV\subseteq\{m:|m|\leq N_{0}\}
. Now take
N>N_{0}+2
, by (
5.18
), we have

\widetilde{C}^{1}_{01}(N+2)=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{1}_{01}(m_{1},m_{2})=\mathcal{C}_{1}<\infty.

This means that by the argument as (2) in Proposition
6.1
, we can derive

(G^{(1)}_{1}f_{N})(N+2)=\frac{i-1}{4}\sum\limits_{k=2}^{2N+2}\frac{1}{k}+\Big(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\mathcal{C}_{1}}{32}\Big)\sum\limits_{k=2}^{2N+2}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k}\rightarrow\infty,\quad N\rightarrow\infty.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\|(G^{(1)}_{1}+G^{(1)}_{2})f_{1}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})}

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{n=N_{0}+2}^{+\infty}\left|\sum\limits_{m=-1}^{1}\Big[\big(a+c(-1)^{n+m}\big)\Big(\frac{1}{n+|m|}+\frac{1}{n-|m|}\Big)+\frac{2bn}{n^{2}+m^{2}}\Big]\right|

\displaystyle:=\sum\limits_{n=N_{0}+2}^{+\infty}\big|G_{a,b,c}(n)\big|,

where the coefficients
a,b,c
are defined as follows:

a=\frac{i-1}{8}=b,\quad c=\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{64}\mathcal{C}_{1}.

A direct calculation yields that

\displaystyle G_{a,b,c}(n)=\frac{\big(6(a+b)-2c(-1)^{n}\big)n^{4}+4((a-b-c(-1)^{n})n^{2}-2(a+b+c(-1)^{n})}{n(n^{2}+1)(n^{2}-1)}.

By means of the triangle inequality and the condition that
\mathcal{C}_{1}\neq 16(1\mp 3\sqrt{2})
, that is,
|3(a+b)|\neq|c|
, then we have

\displaystyle\|(G^{(1)}_{1}+G^{(1)}_{2})f_{1}\|_{\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})}

\displaystyle\geq\sum\limits_{n=N_{0}+2}^{+\infty}\big|G_{a,b,c}(n)\big|\gtrsim\sum\limits_{n=N_{0}+2}^{+\infty}\Big||3(a+b)|-|c|\Big|\cdot\frac{1}{n}+C^{{}^{\prime}}=+\infty,

where
C^{{}^{\prime}}
is a constant. This completes the proof of (
6.10
).

(2)
For the former, by the definition of
\mathcal{B}_{21}
, since
\widetilde{Q}
has the same cancelation property as
\widetilde{S}_{0}
, the proof is completely same as the Step 1 in (1) above apart from the difference in the notation. For the latter, from the expression (
5.21
) of
\widetilde{K}_{-2}(n,m)
and the definition of
\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01}
, we have

\displaystyle(K_{P_{1}}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{-2})(n,m)

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{i-1}{8}+(-1)^{n+m}\Big(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{\widetilde{C}^{2}_{01}(n)}{64}+\frac{\widetilde{C}_{-2}(n)}{32}\Big)\Big)\Big(\frac{1}{|n|+|m|}+\frac{1}{|n|-|m|}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{E}(n,m)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(i-1)|n|}{4(n^{2}+m^{2})}\mathbf{1}_{E}(n,m)+R^{(2)}(n,m),

where
R^{(2)}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}))
for all
1\leq p\leq\infty
,
\widetilde{C}_{-2}(n)
is defined in (
5.22
) and
\widetilde{C}^{2}_{01}(n)
is defined in (
5.18
) by replacing
\widetilde{S}_{0}B^{1}_{01}
with
\widetilde{Q}B^{2}_{01}
. Using an analogue argument as Step 2 in (1), the desired results can be also derived, for brevity, we omit the details and finish the whole proof.
∎

This proposition, together with Proposition
6.1
thus gives the proof of Theorem
1.4
for the case where
0
is a regular point of
H
. Next, we turn to the remaining two resonant cases.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
and
V
be compactly supported. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
and
{\bm{0}}

is a first kind resonance of

{\bm{H}}
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)
If
\mathcal{C}_{3}\neq 0
, then for any
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
6.2
),
\mathcal{K}
is unbounded on
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
,
where the constant
\mathcal{C}_{3}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{3}=\frac{i-1}{8}-\frac{i}{64}C_{-1}+\frac{1}{32}C_{02}+\frac{iC_{11}}{32}+\frac{iC_{12}}{32}-\frac{C_{21}}{16}-\frac{iC_{33}}{16}

(6.11)

with
C_{-1},C_{11},C_{12},C_{33}
defined in (
3.48
) and (
3.51
), respectively and

\displaystyle C_{02}=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1}QA^{1}_{02}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}),\quad C_{21}=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1}QA^{1}_{21}v)(m_{1},m_{2}).

(2)
Let
C_{02},C_{11},C_{12},C_{21},C_{33}
be as in (
6.11
) and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
be as in (
6.7
) and (
6.8
), respectively. Define

D=\frac{i-1}{4}+\frac{i+1}{32}C_{02}+\frac{i-1}{32}C_{11}+\frac{i}{16}C_{12}-\frac{i+1}{16}C_{21}-\frac{i}{8}C_{33}.

(6.12)

Under the condition that

\displaystyle 192|D|\neq\left\{\begin{aligned} &16,\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{1}\big|,\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{2}\big|,\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(6.13)

the corresponding
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
6.2
) is unbounded on
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

When
0
is a first kind resonance of
H
, recalling from (
6.2
) that

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=K_{P_{1}}+\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{11}}K_{A}+\left\{\begin{aligned} &K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}},\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\widetilde{K}_{-1}+\widetilde{K}_{01}+\widetilde{K}_{02},\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}}+\sum\limits_{B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}}K_{B},\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H.\end{aligned}\right.

Based on Propositions
6.1
and
6.2
above, we first observe the following two facts.

•

For the operator in the low energy part
\mathcal{K}_{1}
, recalling the operators
\{K_{A}:A\in\mathcal{A}_{11}\}

from (
3.46
) and (
3.49
), we have

K_{P_{1}}+\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}_{11}}K_{A}=K_{P_{1}}+K_{-1}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}(K_{0j}+K_{2j})+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{3}K_{1j}+K_{33},

(6.14)

where
K_{13}
is the integral operator with the kernel as (
3.4
) by replacing
A_{1}
with
A^{1}_{13}
. By applying the method used for
K_{1}
in Proposition
6.1
to
K=K_{01},K_{13},K_{22}
, we conclude that

\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|Kf_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty\ and\ Kf_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}),\quad K=K_{01},K_{13},K_{22}.

•

For the operator in the high energy part
\mathcal{K}_{3}
, we have

\sup\limits_{N\in\mathbb{N}^{+}}\|Kf_{N}\|_{\ell^{\infty}}<\infty\ and\ Kf_{1}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}),

where

\displaystyle K\in\left\{\begin{aligned} &\emptyset,\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&\{\widetilde{K}_{-1},\widetilde{K}_{02}\},\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&\{K_{B}:B\in\mathcal{B}_{21}\setminus\{\widetilde{K}_{-2},\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01}\}\},\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H.\end{aligned}\right.

Denote

\mathcal{K}_{0}:=K_{P_{1}}+K_{-1}+K_{02}+K_{21}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}K_{1j}+K_{33}.

Then the analysis of
\mathcal{K}
above reduces to
\mathcal{K}_{r}
, where

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{r}=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathcal{K}_{0}+K_{\widetilde{P}_{1}},\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&\mathcal{K}_{0}+\widetilde{K}_{01},\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&\mathcal{K}_{0}+\widetilde{K}_{-2}+\widetilde{\widetilde{K}}_{01},\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H.\end{aligned}\right.

(6.15)

By a similar argument to that used in part (2) of Proposition
6.2
, the desired conclusion follows.
∎

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
and
V
be compactly supported. Suppose that
H
has no positive eigenvalues in the interval
\rm{(}0,16\rm{)}
and
{\bm{0}}

is a second kind resonance of

{\bm{H}}
. Then the following statements hold:

(1)
If
\mathcal{C}_{4}\neq 0
, then for any
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
6.3
),
\mathcal{K}
is unbounded on
\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})
,
where the constant
\mathcal{C}_{4}
is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{4}=\frac{i-1}{8}-\frac{i}{64}C_{-1,3}+\frac{1}{32}C_{03}+\frac{iC^{*}_{11}}{32}+\frac{iC^{*}_{12}}{32}-\frac{C^{*}_{21}}{16}-\frac{iC^{*}_{33}}{16}-\frac{C_{-2,1}}{32}+\frac{C^{(2)}_{01}}{32}

(6.16)

with
C^{*}_{ij}
defined in (
3.51
) by replacing
A^{1}_{ij}
with
A^{2}_{ij}
and

\displaystyle C_{-1,3}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{2}S_{0}A_{-1,3}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}),\quad C_{03}=\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{1}QA^{2}_{03}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}),

\displaystyle C_{-2,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{6}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{3}S_{2}A_{-2,1}S_{0}v_{2})(m_{1},m_{2}),\quad C^{(2)}_{01}=\frac{1}{3}\sum\limits_{m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}}(v_{3}S_{2}A^{2}_{01}v)(m_{1},m_{2}).

(2)
Let
C_{-1,3},C_{03},C^{*}_{11},C^{*}_{12},C^{*}_{21},C^{*}_{33},C_{-2,1},C^{(2)}_{01}
be as in (
6.16
) and
\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}
be as in (
6.7
) and (
6.8
), respectively. Define

E=\frac{i-1}{4}+\frac{i+1}{32}C_{03}+\frac{i-1}{32}C^{*}_{11}+\frac{i}{16}C^{*}_{12}-\frac{i+1}{16}C^{*}_{21}-\frac{i}{8}C^{*}_{33}.+\frac{i-1}{32}C_{-2,1}+\frac{1-i}{32}C^{(2)}_{01}.

(6.17)

Under the condition that

\displaystyle 192|E|\neq\left\{\begin{aligned} &16,\ 16\ is\ a\ regular\ point\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{1}\big|,\ 16\ is\ a\ resonance\ of\ H,\\
&\big|16-\mathcal{C}_{2}\big|,\ 16\ is\ an\ eigenvalue\ of\ H,\end{aligned}\right.

(6.18)

the corresponding
\mathcal{K}
defined in (
6.3
) is unbounded on
\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

Compared (
6.2
) with (
6.3
), the difference lies in the part
\{K_{A}:A\in\mathcal{A}_{21}\}
, where
A_{21}=\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{21}\cup\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{21}
by (
3.52
).
Note that the operators
\{K_{A}:A\in\mathcal{A}^{(2)}_{21}\}
essentially the same as
\{K_{A}:A\in\mathcal{A}_{11}\}
, apart from the difference in the notation. Therefore, in this case, more attention should be paid to the additional operators
\{K_{A}:A\in\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{21}\}
, compared to the first kind resonant case.

From Proposition
3.10
and (
3.54
), we further obtain

\sum\limits_{A\in\mathcal{A}^{(1)}_{21}}K_{A}=K_{-3}+\sum\limits_{j=1}^{2}(K_{-2,j}+K_{-1,j}+K^{(2)}_{0j}).

It can be observed that the following terms in the integral kernels appear newly compared to the previous two cases:

•

\mathcal{T}_{1}(n,m)=\big<(S_{2}A_{-3}S_{2}\varphi_{m})(\cdot),|n-\cdot|v(\cdot)\big>h_{0,-1}(n,m)
,

•

\mathcal{T}_{2}(n,m)=\big<(S_{2}A_{-2,1}S_{0}v_{2})(\cdot),|n-\cdot|v(\cdot)\big>g_{0,-i}(n,m)
,

•

\mathcal{T}_{3}(n,m)=\big<(S_{2}A_{-1,1}Q\widetilde{\varphi}_{m})(\cdot),|n-\cdot|v(\cdot)\big>h_{0,1}(n,m)
,

•

\mathcal{T}_{4}(n,m)=\big<(S_{2}A^{2}_{01}v)(\cdot),|n-\cdot|v(\cdot)\big>g_{0,i}(n,m).

Obviously, it follows from (
6.4
), (
6.6
) and the uniform boundedness of the inner product that

\mathcal{T}_{2}
and
\mathcal{T}_{4}
are
\ell^{\infty}
bounded. More interestingly, under the assumption that
suppV\subseteq\{m:|m|\leq N_{0}\}
for some integer
N_{0}
, when we consider the characteristic function as test function, we can prove that for
N>N_{0}
,

\mathcal{T}_{j}f_{N}(N+2)=0\ {\rm and}\ \|\mathcal{T}_{j}f_{1}\|_{\ell^{1}}<\infty,\quad 1\leq j\leq 4.

To see this, we consider
\mathcal{T}_{1}
only for simplicity. When
\pm n>N_{0}
, note that

\big<(S_{2}A_{-3}S_{2}\varphi_{m})(\cdot),|n-\cdot|v(\cdot)\big>h_{0,-1}(n,m)=\pm\big<(S_{2}A_{-3}S_{2}\varphi_{m})(\cdot),(n-\cdot)v(\cdot)\big>h_{0,-1}(n,m)=0,

where the last equality follows from the orthogonality
\big<S_{2}f,v_{j}\big>=0
for
j=0,1
.
This immediately yields that
\mathcal{T}_{1}f_{N}(N+2)=0
for
N>N_{0}
. Regarding
\|T_{1}f_{1}\|_{\ell^{1}}
, using the uniform boundedness of
h_{0,-1}(n,m)
and
\varphi_{m}
, we obtain

\|\mathcal{T}_{1}f_{1}\|_{\ell^{1}}\leq\Big(\sum\limits_{|n|\leq N_{0}}+\sum\limits_{|n|>N_{0}}\Big)\sum\limits_{m=-1}^{1}|\mathcal{T}_{1}(n,m)|=\sum\limits_{|n|\leq N_{0}}\sum\limits_{m=-1}^{1}|\mathcal{T}_{1}(n,m)|<\infty.

This shows that these newly emerged terms behave well under such test functions. A similar analysis as in the first kind resonance case can therefore be applied, for brevity, we omit the details.
∎

Summing up Propositions
6.1
\sim
6.4
, we consequently complete the whole proof of Theorem
1.4
.

7.
Application

As an application of Theorem
1.3
, in this section, we will establish the
\ell^{p}-\ell^{p^{\prime}}
decay estimates for the solution to the discrete beam equation with parameter
a\in\mathbb{R}
on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} &(\partial_{tt}u)(t,n)+[(\Delta^{2}+V+a^{2})u](t,n)=0,\ \ (t,n)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z},\\
&u(0,n)=\varphi_{1}(n),\ \left(\partial_{t}u\right)(0,n)=\varphi_{2}(n),\end{aligned}\right.

whose solution can be expressed as

u_{a}(t,n)={\rm cos}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+V+a^{2}})\varphi_{1}(n)+\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+V+a^{2}})}{\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+V+a^{2}}}\varphi_{2}(n).

More precisely, we have

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
H=\Delta^{2}+V
satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
1.3
. Let
1<p\leq 2
and
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
. Then for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
,

\|{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}+\left\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{H+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{H+a^{2}}}P_{ac}(H)\right\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}(\frac{1}{p}-\frac{1}{p^{\prime}})},\quad t\neq 0.

(7.1)

To derive this theorem, using the interwining property (
1.4
) and the
\ell^{p^{\prime}}
boundedness of
W_{\pm}
, for any
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
j=1,2
, we obtain

\|f_{a,j}(H)P_{ac}(H)\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\leq\|W_{\pm}\|_{\ell^{p^{\prime}}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\|f_{a,j}(\Delta^{2})\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}}\|W^{*}_{\pm}\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p}}\lesssim\|f_{a,j}(\Delta^{2})\|_{\ell^{p}\rightarrow\ell^{p^{\prime}}},

where

f_{a,1}(x)={\rm cos}(t\sqrt{x+a^{2}}),\quad f_{a,2}(x)=\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{x+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{x+a^{2}}}.

Consequently, it reduces to establish the corresponding estimates for the free propagators
f_{a,j}(\Delta^{2})
with
j=1,2
. To this end, it suffices to establish the following
\ell^{1}-\ell^{\infty}
decay estimate.

Lemma 7.2
.

For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
and
t\neq 0
, we have

\big\|{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}})\big\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}\right\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}.

(7.2)

Once this lemma is proved, based on
\|e^{-it\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}\|_{\ell^{2}\rightarrow\ell^{2}}=1
and the relations

{\rm cos}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}})=\frac{e^{-it\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}+e^{it\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}}{2},\quad\frac{{\rm sin}(t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}})}{t\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}=\frac{1}{2t}\int_{-t}^{t}{\rm cos}\left(s\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}\right)ds,

(7.3)

the desired (
7.1
) for the free case then follows by the Riesz-Thorin interpolation theorem.

Remark 7.3
.

We point out that the sharp decay estimate
|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}
is not affected by the values of parameter
a
, which is quite different from its continuous counterpart where it is influenced by
a
. For instance, the continuous analogue of (
7.2
) exhibits a decay rate of
|t|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
when
a=0
, whereas for
a=1
, the decay is
|t|^{-\frac{1}{4}}
in the low-energy part and
|t|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in the high-energy part. For more details, we refer to
[
14
]
.

Proof of Lemma
7.2
.

For any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, from (
7.3
) above, the problem reduces to proving

\big\|e^{-it\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}\big\|_{\ell^{1}\rightarrow\ell^{\infty}}\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}},\quad t\neq 0.

(7.4)

When
a=0
, since such sharp
\ell^{1}-\ell^{\infty}
decay estimate was established in
[
51
]
, here we focus on the case
a\neq 0
. Indeed, by virtue of Fourier transform (
2.1
), the kernel of
e^{-it\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}
is given by

\big(e^{-it\sqrt{\Delta^{2}+a^{2}}}\big)(n,m)=(2\pi)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-it\sqrt{(2-2{\rm cos}\theta)^{2}+a^{2}}}e^{i(n-m)\theta}d\theta.

We claim that the following estimate holds:

\sup\limits_{s\in\mathbb{R}}\Big|\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}e^{-it\big[\sqrt{(2-2{\rm cos}\theta)^{2}+a^{2}}-s\theta\big]}d\theta\Big|\lesssim|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}},\quad t\neq 0.

(7.5)

To establish this estimate, it suffices to consider the interval
[-\pi,0]
, as the estimate on
[0,\pi]
follows by the change of variable
\theta\mapsto-\theta
. For any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\Phi_{a,s}(\theta)=\sqrt{(2-2{\rm cos}\theta)^{2}+a^{2}}-s\theta,\quad\theta\in[-\pi,0].

A direct computation yields that

\Phi^{\prime}_{a,s}(\theta)=4\big((2-2{\rm cos}\theta)^{2}+a^{2}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-{\rm cos}\theta){\rm sin}\theta-s

and

\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{a,s}(\theta)=4\big((2-2{\rm cos}\theta)^{2}+a^{2}\big)^{-\frac{3}{2}}(1-{\rm cos}\theta)\big(4{\rm cos^{3}}\theta-8{\rm cos^{2}}\theta+(2a^{2}+4){\rm cos}\theta+a^{2}\big).

Let

h_{a}(x):=4x^{3}-8x^{2}+(2a^{2}+4)x+a^{2},\quad x\in[-1,1].

We observe that
h_{a}(x)>0
for
x\geq 0
,
h_{a}(-1)=-a^{2}-16<0
and
h^{\prime}_{a}(x)>0
for
x<0
. Let
x_{0}
denote the unique root of
h_{a}(x)
in the interval
[-1,1]
. Then

\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{a,s}(\theta)=0\Leftrightarrow\theta=0\ {\rm or}\ \theta=\theta_{0}\in(-\pi,-\frac{\pi}{2}),\quad{\rm where\ cos}\theta_{0}=x_{0}.

This implies that
\Phi^{\prime}_{a,s}(\theta)
is monotonically decreasing on
[-\pi,\theta_{0}]
and increasing on
[\theta_{0},0]
. Combining this with
\Phi^{\prime}_{a,s}(-\pi)=-s=\Phi^{\prime}_{a,s}(0)
, we conclude that for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, the equation
\Phi^{\prime}_{a,s}(\theta)=0
has at most two solutions on
[-\pi,0]
. By Van der Corput lemma (see e.g.
[
52
, P.
{332-334}
]
), the slower decay rates of the oscillatory integral (
7.5
) on
[-\pi,0]
occur in the cases of
s=0
and
s=s_{0}
, and for the other values of
s
, the decay rate is either
|t|^{-1}
or
|t|^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, where
s_{0}=4\big((2-2{\rm cos}\theta_{0})^{2}+a^{2}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-{\rm cos}\theta_{0}){\rm sin}\theta_{0}
.

If
s=0
, then

\Phi^{\prime}_{a,0}(\theta)=0\Leftrightarrow\theta=0\ {\rm or}\ \theta=-\pi.

Moreover, we can compute

\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{a,0}(-\pi)\neq 0,\quad\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{a,0}(0)=0\ {\rm but}\ \Phi^{(3)}_{a,0}(0)\neq 0,

thus by Van der Corput lemma, the decay rate is
|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}
.

If
s=s_{0}
, then
\Phi^{\prime}_{a,s_{0}}(\theta)=0\Leftrightarrow\theta=\theta_{0}.
And
\Phi^{\prime\prime}_{a,s_{0}}(\theta_{0})=0
but
\Phi^{(3)}_{a,s_{0}}(\theta_{0})\neq 0
, then the decay rate is
|t|^{-\frac{1}{3}}
. In summary, this completes the proof of (
7.5
), from which (
7.4
) follows.
∎

Appendix A
Discrete Calderón Zygmund operators on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

The study of harmonic analysis in the discrete setting, particularly concerning singular integrals, has a long history. As a typical model of discrete singular integral, the discrete Hilbert transform was first introduced by D. Hilbert and proven to be bounded on
\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})
for
1<p<\infty
by M. Riesz
[
49
]
as a consequence of his proof for the continuous case on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R})
. Subsequent developments can be traced through the works of Calderón-Zygmund
[
15
]
, Stein-Wainger
[
54
]
, Lust-Piquard
[
40
]
, Laeng
[
39
]
, Pierce
[
47
]
and Krause
[
38
]
, among others. Notably, in recent work
[
7
]
by Bañuelos, Kim and Kwaśnicki, they established the
\ell^{p}
boundedness of discrete analogues of classical convolution-type Calderón-Zygmund operators for
1<p<\infty
. The idea of their work is that the
\ell^{p}
norm of such discrete operators can be controlled by the
L^{p}
norm of their continuous counterparts.

Following this idea, this appendix is devoted to extending the results of
[
7
, Proposition 6.1]
to the discrete non-convolution type Calderón-Zygmund operators on the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Let
T
be a linear operator acting on the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We say that
T
is a
Calderón-Zygmund operator
if it is bounded on
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and admits the integral representation:

\displaystyle(Tf)(x)=p.v.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}K(x,y)f(y)dy,

(A.1)

where the kernel
K\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{(x,x):x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\})
and satisfies

\displaystyle|K(x,y)|\lesssim|x-y|^{-d},\quad|(\partial_{x}K)(x,y)|+|(\partial_{y}K)(x,y)|\lesssim|x-y|^{-(d+1)},\quad x\neq y.

(A.2)

It is well-known that such operators extend to bounded linear operators on
L^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
1<p<\infty
(see
[
24
, Chapter 4]
). We consider its discrete analogue
T_{{\rm dis}}
defined by

\displaystyle(T_{{\rm dis}}f)(n)=\sum\limits_{m\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\setminus{\{n\}}}K(n,m)f(m),\quad f\in\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}).

(A.3)

By virtue of the idea of
[
7
]
, we can establish the following conclusion.

Theorem A.1
.

Let
T
and
T_{{\rm dis}}
be defined as above. Then we have
T_{{\rm dis}}\in\mathbb{B}(\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z}^{d}))
for
1<p<\infty
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we focus on
d=1
and the cases
d\geq 2
can be obtained similarly. For any
1<p<\infty
, let
f\in\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})
and
g\in\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z})
with
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Given
x\in\mathbb{R}
, there exist unique
n\in\mathbb{Z}
and
x_{0}\in U=[0,1)
such that
x=n+x_{0}
. We then define
F(x)=f(n)
,
G(x)=g(n)
, which immediately yields that
\|F\|_{L^{p}(\mathbb{R})}=\|f\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})}
and
\|G\|_{L^{q}(\mathbb{R})}=\|g\|_{\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z})}
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\left<TF,G\right>

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}K(x,y)F(y)\overline{G(x)}dydx=\sum\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{n+U}\int_{m+U}K(x,y)dydxf(m)\overline{g(n)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}\Big(\underbrace{\int_{n+U}\int_{m+U}K(x,y)dydx-K(n,m)}_{\widetilde{K}(n,m)}+K(n,m)\Big)f(m)\overline{g(n)}

\displaystyle=\sum\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{K}}(n,m)f(m)\overline{g(n)}+\left<T_{{\rm dis}}f,g\right>.

Through variable substitution and the differential mean value theorem, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\widetilde{K}(n,m)

\displaystyle=\int_{U}\int_{U}(K(x+n,y+m)-K(n,m))dxdy

\displaystyle=\int_{U}\int_{U}\Big(x\partial_{x}K(n+x\theta,m+y\theta)+y\partial_{y}K(n+x\theta,m+y\theta)\Big)dxdy,

for some
\theta\in[0,1]
. Under the smoothness condition (
A.2
), we have the decay estimate:

\displaystyle|\widetilde{K}(n,m)|\lesssim|n-m|^{-2},\quad|n-m|\gg 1.

Applying Hölder’s inequality yields

\displaystyle\Big|\sum\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}{\widetilde{K}}(n,m)f(m)\overline{g(n)}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big(\sum\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\widetilde{K}}(n,m)|\cdot|f(m)|^{p}\Big)^{\frac{1}{p}}\Big(\sum\limits_{n,m\in\mathbb{Z}}|{\widetilde{K}}(n,m)|\cdot|g(n)|^{q}\Big)^{\frac{1}{q}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{\ell^{p}(\mathbb{Z})}\|g\|_{\ell^{q}(\mathbb{Z})}.

Hence, using
T\in\mathbb{B}(L^{p}(\mathbb{R}))
for
1<p<\infty
and triangle inequality, the desired result is obtained.
∎
